\mathcal{U}=\{u_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
, and denote
\mathcal{U}_{-}=\{u_{k}-(a,a):k\in\mathbb{Z},a\in\mathbb{N}\}
,
\mathcal{U}_{+}=\{u_{k}+(a,a):k\in\mathbb{Z},a\in\mathbb{N}\}
. Let
\mathcal{U}_{c}
contain all
u\in\mathcal{U}_{+}
such that
\{u-(1,0),u-(0,1)\}\not\subset\mathcal{U}_{+}
. Assume that
\mathbf{0}\in\mathcal{U}\cup\mathcal{U}_{-}
.

We now consider the event
I_{t}=\mathcal{U}\cup\mathcal{U}_{-}
. It is equivalent to that
\mathbf{G}(u_{k})\leq t
for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
\mathbf{G}(u)>t
(or equivalently
\xi^{\vee}(u)>t-\mathbf{G}(u-(1,0))\vee\mathbf{G}(u-(0,1))
), for any
u\in\mathcal{U}_{c}
.
We next study the distribution of
\{\xi^{\vee}(u)\}_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{+}}
, conditioned on this event.

By the first two properties in Section
3.1
, we know that
\{\mathbf{G}(u)\}_{u\in\mathcal{U}\cup\mathcal{U}_{-}}
determines
\{\mathbf{B}(u_{k},u_{k-1})\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\{\xi(u)\}_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{-}}
.
We next show that
\{\mathbf{G}(u)\}_{u\in\mathcal{U}\cup\mathcal{U}_{-}}
is also determined by
\{\mathbf{B}(u_{k},u_{k-1})\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\{\xi(u)\}_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{-}}
.
Indeed, by the first property in Section
3.1
, for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
we have that
\mathbf{G}(u_{k})-\mathbf{G}(u_{0})=\mathbf{B}(u_{0},u_{k})
is determined by
\{\mathbf{B}(u_{k},u_{k-1})\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
.
Then using the second property in Section
3.1
, and the fact that
\mathbf{0}\in\mathcal{U}\cup\mathcal{U}_{-}
, we have that for any
u\in\mathcal{U}\cup\mathcal{U}_{-}
,
\mathbf{G}(u)-\mathbf{G}(u_{0})
is determined by
\{\mathbf{B}(u_{k},u_{k-1})\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\{\xi(u)\}_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{-}}
, in particular for
u=\mathbf{0}
. Since
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{0})=0
, we have that
\mathbf{G}(u_{0})
, thus
\mathbf{G}(u)
for any
u\in\mathcal{U}\cup\mathcal{U}_{-}
is determined by
\{\mathbf{B}(u_{k},u_{k-1})\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\{\xi(u)\}_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{-}}
.

By the last three properties of the Busemann function
\mathbf{B}
in Section
3.1
,
\{\mathbf{B}(u_{k},u_{k-1})\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
,
\{\xi(u)\}_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{-}}
, and
\{\xi^{\vee}(u)\}_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{+}}
are independent exponential random variables.
Thus conditioned on
\{\mathbf{G}(u)\}_{u\in\mathcal{U}\cup\mathcal{U}_{-}}
and the event
I_{t}=\mathcal{U}\cup\mathcal{U}_{-}
, we have

•

\{\xi^{\vee}(u)\}_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{+}}
are independent random variables,

•

\xi^{\vee}(u)\sim\Exp(1)
for each
u\in\mathcal{U}_{+}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{c}
,

•

for each
u\in\mathcal{U}_{c}
,
\xi^{\vee}(u)
has the distribution of
\Exp(1)
conditioned on
>t-\mathbf{G}(u-(1,0))\vee\mathbf{G}(u-(0,1))
.

Since an
\Exp(1)
random variable conditioned on
>x
for any
x\geq 0
is just
x+\Exp(1)
, we have that

\xi^{\vee}(u)-(t-\mathbf{G}(u-(1,0))\vee\mathbf{G}(u-(0,1)))\sim\Exp(1)

for each
u\in\mathcal{U}_{c}
.
We note that (still conditioned on
\{\mathbf{G}(u)\}_{u\in\mathcal{U}\cup\mathcal{U}_{-}}
and the event
I_{t}=\mathcal{U}\cup\mathcal{U}_{-}
) we have
\xi^{\vee,t}(u)=\xi^{\vee}(u)
for any
u\in\mathcal{U}_{+}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{c}
and
\xi^{\vee,t}(u)=\xi^{\vee}(u)-(t-\mathbf{G}(u-(1,0))\vee\mathbf{G}(u-(0,1)))
, so
\{\xi^{\vee,t}(u)\}_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{+}}
are i.i.d.
\Exp(1)
random variables. Thus the conclusion follows.
∎

For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
u\not\in I_{t}
, the path
\Gamma^{\vee}_{u}\setminus I_{t}
can be constructed as the geodesic with boundary
I_{t}
, under the weights
\xi^{\vee,t}
.
For any
u\leq v
,
u,v\not\in I_{t}
, let
T^{\vee,t}_{u,v}
and
\Gamma^{\vee,t}_{u,v}
denote the passage time and geodesic from
u
to
v
under the weights
\xi^{\vee,t}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

For any
v\not\in I_{t}
we have
\mathbf{G}(v)-t=\max_{u\leq v,u\not\in I_{t}}T^{\vee,t}_{u,v}
and
\Gamma^{\vee}_{v}\setminus I_{t}=\Gamma^{\vee,t}_{u_{*},v}
for
u_{*}=\argmax_{u\leq v,u\not\in I_{t}}T^{\vee,t}_{u,v}
.

The proof of this lemma is by a straightforward induction in
u
in the up-right direction, and we omit the details here.

3.3
The coupling and the competition interface

\mathbf{0}
p_{t}
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
Z

Figure 3
:

An illustration of the growth process from LPP: the blue and green areas are the two clusters
C_{1}\cap I_{t}
and
C_{2}\cap I_{t}
respectively, and the red curve is the semi-infinite geodesic
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
.

We now describe the coupling between LPP and TASEP (denoted as a Markov process on
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
).
In this subsection we let
(\eta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
denote TASEP with the following initial condition: let
\eta_{0}(0)=0
and
\eta_{0}(1)=1
, and
\eta_{0}(x)
be i.i.d. Bernoulli
(\rho)
for all other
x
.
We label the holes by
\mathbb{Z}
from left to right, with the one at site
0
labeled
0
;
and label the particles by
\mathbb{Z}
from right to left, with the one at site
1
labeled
0
.
For any
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, if at time
0
the particle labeled
b
is to the right of the hole labeled
a
, we denote
L(a,b)=0
; otherwise, we denote
L(a,b)>0
as the time when the particle switches with the hole.
Then we have that
\{L(a,b)\}_{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
has the same distribution as
\{\mathbf{G}(a,b)\vee 0\}_{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
.
Indeed, using the last property of the Busemann function in Section
3.1
, we can deduce that
I_{0}
and
\{(a,b):L(a,b)=0\}
have the same distribution; and given
\eta_{0}
, the random variables

L(a,b)-L(a-1,b)\vee L(a,b-1)

for all
(a,b)
with
L(a,b)>0
are i.i.d.
\Exp(1)
, because this is the waiting time for the particle labeled
b
and the hole labeled
a
to switch since the time they are next to each other.
Thus they have the same distribution as
\{\xi^{\vee,0}(u)\}_{u\not\in I_{0}}
conditioned on
I_{0}
, according to Lemma
3.4
.
See e.g.
[
FMP09
, Section 4.2]
for more details on the equal in distribution between
L
and
\mathbf{G}\vee 0
.
We couple
(\eta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
with LPP so that
L=\mathbf{G}\vee 0
almost surely, and in the rest of this section we work under the event that this equality holds.
Then the TASEP configuration
\eta_{t}
can be directly read from
I_{t}
(see Figure
4
).

\mathbf{0}

Figure 4
:

An illustration of the correspondence between TASEP and the growth process.

Lemma 3.6
.

For any
t\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\eta_{t}(x)=0
if and only if there is some
y\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
(x+y,y)\in I_{t}
and
(x+y,y+1)\not\in I_{t}
, and the hole at site
x
has label
x+y
; and
\eta_{t}(x)=1
if and only if there is some
y\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
(x+y-1,y)\in I_{t}
and
(x+y,y)\not\in I_{t}
, and the particle at site
x
has label
y
.
Equivalently, if we let
f_{t}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
be the function such that
f_{t}(x)
is the largest integer with
(f_{t}(x)+x,f_{t}(x))\in I_{t}
, then
f_{t}(x-1)-f_{t}(x)=\eta_{t}(x)
.

Proof.

For simplicity of notations we denote
\mathcal{E}_{1}
as the event where there is
y\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
(x+y,y)\in I_{t}
and
(x+y,y+1)\not\in I_{t}
, and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
as the event where there is
y\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
(x+y-1,y)\in I_{t}
and
(x+y,y)\not\in I_{t}
.
Note that exactly one of
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
happens,
so it suffices to show that
\mathcal{E}_{1}
implies
\eta_{t}(x)=0
, since by symmetry we would have that
\mathcal{E}_{2}
implies
\eta_{t}(x)=1
, then the conclusion follows.

If
(x+y,y)\in I_{t}
and
(x+y,y+1)\not\in I_{t}
, we have
L(x+y,y)\leq t
and
L(x+y,y+1)>t
under the coupling.
This means that at time
t
, the hole labeled
x+y
is to the left of the particle labeled
y
, but to the right of the particle labeled
y+1
.
Suppose that at time
0
, the hole labeled
x+y
is at site
z
.
Since the hole at site
0
is labeled
0
, if
x+y>0
we must have
z>1
, and there are
z-(x+y)
particles between sites
0
and
z
; and if
x+y<0
we must have
z<0
, and there are
(x+y)-z
particles between sites
z
and
0
.
In either case, the nearest particle to the left of the hole labeled
x+y
(at time
0
) must be labeled
x+y-z+1
(since the particle at site
1
is labeled
0
).
This means that at time
t
, the hole labeled
x+y
has already swapped with
(y+1)-(x+y-z+1)=z-x
particles. So at time
t
it is at site
x
, and
\eta_{t}(x)=0
.
∎

We next consider the semi-infinite geodesic
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
under this coupling.
It actually corresponds to the competition interface starting from
\mathbf{0}
, which we describe now (see e.g.
[
FP05
,
FMP09
]
).
We define two clusters
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
for the growth process
(I_{t})_{t\geq 0}
: let
\mathbb{Z}_{+}\times\{0\}\subset C_{1}
, and
\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}\subset C_{2}
.
For any
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
let its ‘parent’ be either
(a-1,b)
or
(a,b-1)
, whichever is occupied later;
then
(a,b)
is in the same cluster as its parent.
Starting from any
u
and by taking parent repeatedly, we can actually get
\Gamma_{u}^{\vee}\setminus I_{0}
, by (
3.1
); thus we can equivalently define
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
such that for any
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
,
u\neq\mathbf{0}
, we let
u\in C_{1}
if
\Gamma^{\vee}_{u}
intersects
\mathbb{Z}_{+}\times\{0\}
, and
u\in C_{2}
if
\Gamma^{\vee}_{u}
intersects
\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.
By Lemma
3.5
, such clusters are determined by
I_{0}
and
\{\xi^{\vee,0}(u)\}_{u\not\in I_{0}}
, which are i.i.d.
\Exp(1)
conditioned on
I_{0}
.
The competition interface
Z
is defined to be the boundary of these clusters
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
. Namely, we let
Z\subset(1/2,1/2)+\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, such that for any
v\in Z
, every vertex in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
to the upper-left of
v
is in
C_{2}
, and every vertex in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
to the lower-right of
v
is in
C_{1}
.
By Lemma
3.3
,
Z=\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}+(1/2,1/2)
.
In words, the competition interface
Z
defined from
I_{0}
and
\{\xi^{\vee,0}(u)\}_{u\not\in I_{0}}
is equivalent to the semi-infinite geodesic
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
defined from
\{\xi(u)\}_{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
.
We also define the process
(p_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, such that
p_{t}
is the last vertex in
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}\cap I_{t}

(see Figure
3
).

In the TASEP side, in
(\eta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
we keep track of a ‘hole-particle pair’, which is a hole with a particle next to it in the right.
At
t=0
it is the hole at site
0
and particle at site
1
.
Whenever the particle is switched with a hole to the right, we move this pair to the right; and whenever the hole is switched with a particle to the left, we move this pair to the left (see Figure
5
for an illustration).
We have the following lemma from
[
FP05
]
, which says that the trajectory of this ‘hole-particle pair’ can be mapped to the competition interface.

Figure 5
:

An illustration of the evolution of a hole-particle pair in
(\eta_{t})_{\geq 0}
: the numbers above the particles/holes are the labels, which increase from left to right for holes, and decrease from left to right for particles. The yellow boxes indicate the tracked hole-particle pairs.

Lemma 3.7
.

Under the above coupling between LPP and TASEP, for the hole-particle pair at time
t
, let
b_{t}
be the label of the particle and
a_{t}
be the label of the hole. Then
p_{t}=(a_{t},b_{t})
.

We note that this hole-particle pair can also be replaced by a second-class particle.
For this, note that
a_{t}
is also the number of times the pair moved to the right up to time
t
, and
b_{t}
is the number of times that pair moved to the left up to time
t
. Thus at time
t
the hole-particle pair is at sites
a_{t}-b_{t}
and
a_{t}-b_{t}+1
.
If we take
\eta_{t}^{*}(x)=\eta_{t}(x)
for
x<a_{t}-b_{t}
,
\eta_{t}^{*}(x)=\eta_{t}(x+1)
for
x>a_{t}-b_{t}
, and
\eta_{t}^{*}(a_{t}-b_{t})=*
, we then have that
(\eta_{t}^{*})_{t\geq 0}
is TASEP with a second-class particle, starting from i.i.d. Bernoulli
(\rho)
on
\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
.
So far we have seen that this process
(\eta_{t}^{*})_{t\geq 0}
contains the same information as
\mathbf{G}\vee 0
(by Lemma
3.6
), thus the same information as
I_{0}
and
\{\xi^{\vee,0}(u)\}_{u\not\in I_{0}}
, and the trajectory of the second-class particle gives the semi-infinite geodesic
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
(Lemma
3.7
).
Recall from Section
2
that
\Phi_{t}
is the law of
\eta_{t}^{*}(a_{t}-b_{t}+\cdot)
, and
\Psi
is the stationary distribution of TASEP as seen from an isolated second-class particle.
In light of the convergence of
\Phi_{t}
to
\Psi
as
t\to\infty
, stated in Theorem
1.7
or Proposition
2.7
, the LPP limiting environment measure
\nu
should be constructed from
\Psi
.
We give such construction in the next section.

4
The LPP limiting environment

We are now ready to define
\nu
.
As before, we use
(\zeta_{t}^{*})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
to denote the process of TASEP as seen from an isolated second-class particle, such that each
\zeta_{t}^{*}\sim\Psi
, the stationary distribution defined in Section
2
.
The idea is to construct a growth process from
(\zeta_{t}^{*})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
, then take the environment around the origin. This would give the limiting environment along the geodesic
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
, as seen at a uniform time; i.e. it is the environment as seen from
p_{t}
for a uniform
t
, where (recall that)
p_{t}
is the last vertex in
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}\cap I_{t}
. To get the environment
\nu
, which is as seen from a uniformly chosen vertex, we would do an extra reweighting.

We first replace the second-class particle in
(\zeta_{t}^{*})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
by a hole-particle pair.
Namely, we let
(\zeta_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
be the process such that
\zeta_{t}(x)=\zeta_{t}^{*}(x)
for
x<0
,
\zeta_{t}(x)=\zeta_{t}^{*}(x-1)
for
x>1
, and
\zeta_{t}(0)=0
,
\zeta_{t}(1)=1
.
The process
(\zeta_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is then the stationary process of TASEP as seen from a hole-particle pair.
We use
\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}
to denote the law of this process.

We next describe the procedure of obtaining the environment from
(\zeta_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
.
We give the growth process in terms of the occupation time function, which we also denote by
L
as a slight abuse of notation.
Similar to the i.i.d. Bernoulli initial setting in Section
3.3
, we label the particles from right to left, and the holes from left to right, such that at time
0
the particle at site
1
and the hole at site
0
are both labeled
0
.
Let
L(a,b)
be the time when the particle labeled
b
is switched with the hole labeled
a
.
Unlike the i.i.d. Bernoulli initial setting, here
(\zeta_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is a stationary process and evolves from time
-\infty
to
\infty
, so
L(a,b)
may be negative and is well-defined for all
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
We then use
L
to define the limiting weights and path, which we denote by
\xi
and
\gamma
by slightly abusing these notations within this section.
We define
\xi
via
\xi(a,b)=L(a+1,b)\wedge L(a,b+1)-L(a,b)
, and define
\gamma\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
as the collection of all
(a,b)
, such that there is a time
t
when the particle labeled
b
is at site
1
and the hole labeled
a
is at site
0
in
\eta_{t}
.
We let
\tilde{\nu}
be the measure given by the law of such
(\xi,\gamma)
constructed from
(\zeta_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\sim\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}
.

We next do the reweighting.
We let
\mathbf{\Psi}
be the measure
\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}
conditioned on
L(\mathbf{0})=0
, i.e. we let
d\mathbf{\Psi}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}\frac{\mathds{1}[L(\mathbf{0})>-\epsilon]d\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}{\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}[L(\mathbf{0})>-\epsilon]}
.
As
(\zeta_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
under
\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}
is a Markov process, the limit could be computed as
\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}
conditioned on that there is a jump of the hole-particle pair at time
0
; i.e. we first reweight
\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}
by
\mathds{1}[\zeta_{0_{-}}(2)=0]+\mathds{1}[\zeta_{0_{-}}(-1)=1]
, the events where a jump is allowed, then let the jump happen at time
0
.
More precisely, we can describe
\mathbf{\Psi}
as follows. We have

\mathbf{\Psi}=\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}[\zeta_{0_{-}}(2)=0]\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}+\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}[\zeta_{0_{-}}(-1)=1]\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}}{\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}[\zeta_{0_{-}}(2)=0]+\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}[\zeta_{0_{-}}(-1)=1]}=\frac{(1-\rho)^{2}\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}+\rho^{2}\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}}{(1-\rho)^{2}+\rho^{2}},

where
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}
(resp.
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}
) is
\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}
conditioned on that a jump of the hole-particle pair to the right (resp. to the left) happens at time
0
.
More precisely, we define these measures as follows.
Let
(\zeta_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\sim\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}
, then the negative time part
(\zeta_{t})_{t<0}
has distribution given by

\frac{\mathds{1}[\zeta_{0_{-}}(2)=0]d\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}{\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}[\zeta_{0_{-}}(2)=0]};

and given
\zeta_{0_{-}}
we let
\zeta_{0}
be that
\zeta_{0}(-1)=\zeta_{0}(0)=0
,
\zeta_{0}(1)=1
, and
\zeta_{0}(x)=\zeta_{0_{-}}(x+1)
for any
x\not\in\{-1,0,1\}
; and let
(\zeta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be the Markov process of TASEP as seen from a hole-particle pair starting from
\zeta_{0}
.
Similarly, for
(\zeta_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\sim\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}
, the negative part
(\zeta_{t})_{t<0}
has distribution given by

\frac{\mathds{1}[\zeta_{0_{-}}(-1)=1]d\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}{\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}[\zeta_{0_{-}}(-1)=1]};

and given
\zeta_{0_{-}}
, we have
\zeta_{0}(0)=0
,
\zeta_{0}(1)=\zeta_{0}(2)=1
, and
\zeta_{0}(x)=\zeta_{0_{-}}(x-1)
for any
x\not\in\{0,1,2\}
; and
(\zeta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is the Markov process of TASEP as seen from a hole-particle pair starting from
\zeta_{0}
.

From this construction, the laws of
\zeta_{0}
under
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}
can also be described as follows.
Let
\Psi_{+}
be the law of
\{\zeta^{*}(x)\}_{x\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\Psi_{-}
be the law of
\{\zeta^{*}(-x)\}_{x\in\mathbb{N}}
, for
\zeta^{*}\sim\Psi
.
Under
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}
, there is
\zeta_{0}(-1)=\zeta_{0}(0)=0
,
\zeta_{0}(1)=1
, and
\{\zeta_{0}(x+1)\}_{x\in\mathbb{N}}\sim\Psi_{+}
and
\{\zeta_{0}(-x-1)\}_{x\in\mathbb{N}}\sim\Psi_{-}
, and they are independent.
Under
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}
, there is
\zeta_{0}(0)=0
,
\zeta_{0}(1)=\zeta_{0}(2)=1
, and
\{\zeta_{0}(x+2)\}_{x\in\mathbb{N}}\sim\Psi_{+}
,
\{\zeta_{0}(-x)\}_{x\in\mathbb{N}}\sim\Psi_{-}
, and they are independent.

We define
\nu
as the measure given by the law of
(\xi,\gamma)
, obtained using the procedure above from
(\zeta_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\sim\mathbf{\Psi}
.
By Lemma
4.2
below we can see that
\xi(\mathbf{0})
has exponential tail under
\nu
, so
\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\xi(\mathbf{0})]<\infty
. We then show that
\tilde{\nu}
is
\nu
reweighted by
\xi(\mathbf{0})
.

Lemma 4.1
.

We have
d\tilde{\nu}=\frac{\xi(\mathbf{0})d\nu}{\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\xi(\mathbf{0})]}
.

Let’s explain why such a relation is expected.
Consider the sequence of times
\{L(u)\}_{u\in\gamma}
when the hole-particle pair jumps. Under
\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}
this is a stationary point process in
\mathbb{R}
.
Then
\nu
corresponds to the environment
as seen from the hole-particle at a typical jump time.
On the other hand,

\tilde{\nu}
corresponds to the environment
as seen from the hole-particle at time
0
.
Because of the ‘inspection effect’, this is biased by the length of the interval in the point process containing time
0
, which is
\xi(\mathbf{0})
.

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

For each
s>0
, we let
\mathbf{\Psi}_{-s}
be the measure of
\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}
conditioned on
L(\mathbf{0})=-s
, i.e. let
d\mathbf{\Psi}_{-s}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}\frac{\mathds{1}[-s-\epsilon<L(\mathbf{0})<-s]d\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}{\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}[-s-\epsilon<L(\mathbf{0})<-s]}
.
Note that under
\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}
, almost surely
L(0,1),L(1,0)>0
and
L(\mathbf{0})<0
, since at time
0
the following objects are ordered from left to right: the particle labeled
1
, the hole labeled
0
, the particle labeled
0
, and the hole labeled
1
. So
\mathds{1}[-s-\epsilon<L(\mathbf{0})<-s]=\mathds{1}[-s-\epsilon<L(\mathbf{0})<-s]\mathds{1}[\xi(\mathbf{0})>s]
.
Then since
\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}
is stationary, we have

\mathds{1}[-s-\epsilon<L(\mathbf{0})<-s]d\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}=(\mathds{1}[L(\mathbf{0})>-\epsilon]\mathds{1}[\xi(\mathbf{0})>s]d\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}})\circ\mathscr{T}_{-s},

where
\mathscr{T}_{-s}
is the time translation operator: for any process
P=(P_{w})_{w\in\mathbb{R}}
, we denote
\mathscr{T}_{-s}P
as the process
(P_{-s+w})_{w\in\mathbb{R}}
.
By multiplying
\epsilon^{-1}
and sending
\epsilon\to 0_{+}
, we have

\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}[L(\mathbf{0})=-s]d\mathbf{\Psi}_{-s}=\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}[L(\mathbf{0})=0](\mathds{1}[\xi(\mathbf{0})>s]d\mathbf{\Psi})\circ\mathscr{T}_{-s},

(4.1)

where

\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}[L(\mathbf{0})=-s]=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}\epsilon^{-1}\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}[-s-\epsilon<L(\mathbf{0})<-s],

\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}[L(\mathbf{0})=0]=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}\epsilon^{-1}\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}[L(\mathbf{0})>-\epsilon]

are the probability densities.
By integrating the left-hand side of (
4.1
) over
s>0
we get
d\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}
, under which the law of
(\xi,\gamma)
is
\tilde{\nu}
.
For the right-hand side of (
4.1
), we note that the laws of
(\xi,\gamma)
are the same under
(\mathds{1}[\xi(\mathbf{0})>s]d\mathbf{\Psi})\circ\mathscr{T}_{-s}
or
\mathds{1}[\xi(\mathbf{0})>s]d\mathbf{\Psi}
. So by integrating over
s>0
and taking the law of
(\xi,\gamma)
, we get
\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}[L(\mathbf{0})=0]\xi(\mathbf{0})d\nu
. Thus we conclude that
d\tilde{\nu}=\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}[L(\mathbf{0})=0]\xi(\mathbf{0})d\nu
. Since
\tilde{\nu}
and
\nu
are probability measures, by integrating both sides we get
\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{\mathbf{\Psi}}}[L(\mathbf{0})=0]\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[\xi(\mathbf{0})]=1
, so the conclusion follows.
∎

The above construction allows us to explicitly compute finite dimensional distributions of
\nu
and thus local geodesic statistics (assuming the main results of this paper).
For this rest of this section we illustrate such computations, and prove Propositions
1.5
and
1.6
.

We start with the following computations on the next jump times.

Lemma 4.2
.

For any
h\geq 0
we have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}}[L(1,0)>h]=(1+\rho(1-\rho)h)e^{-(1-\rho)h},\\
&\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}}[L(0,1)>h]=(1+\rho h)e^{-\rho h},\\
&\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}}[L(1,0)>h]=(1+(1-\rho)h)e^{-(1-\rho)h},\\
&\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}}[L(0,1)>h]=(1+\rho(1-\rho)h)e^{-\rho h}.\end{split}

Proof.

Let
D_{+}=\min\{x\geq 1:\zeta_{0}(x+1)=0\}
, the number of
particles between the origin and the leftmost hole at a positive site.
Similarly let
D_{-}=\min\{x\geq 1:\zeta_{0}(-x)=1\}
,
the number of holes to the right of the rightmost particle at a negative site, up to and including the origin.

The distribution of
D_{+}
under
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}

is that of
X_{+}
given by
(
2.4
), while the distribution of
D_{+}

under
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}
is that of
X_{+}+1

(which is the distribution of the sum of two independent Geometric(
1-\rho
)
random variables).

Similarly the distribution of
D_{-}
under
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}

is that of
X_{-}
at (
2.5
), while the distribution
of
D_{-}
under
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}
is that of
X_{-}+1
.

In order for the particle which is at site
1
at time
0

to jump, the hole starting at site
D_{+}+1
must switch
with each of the
D_{+}
particles starting in
\llbracket 1,D_{+}\rrbracket
.
So given
D_{+}
, the distribution of
L(1,0)
is
the sum of
D_{+}
independent
\Exp(1)
random variables;
that is, a Gamma(
D_{+},1)
distribution. A random
variable
V
with Gamma(
k,1
) distribution has
\mathbb{E}[e^{-sV}]=(1+s)^{-k}
,
and from this we obtain, for any
s>-1+\rho
,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}}[e^{-s(L(1,0))}]=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k(1-\rho)^{2}\rho^{k-1}(1+s)^{-k}=\frac{(1+s)(1-\rho)^{2}}{(1+s-\rho)^{2}},

which can be shown to match the expression for
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}}[L(1,0)>h]
given in the statement.

Similarly, in order for the hole which is at site
0
at time
0
to jump,
the particle starting at site
-D_{-}
must switch with each
of the
D_{-}
holes starting in
\llbracket-D_{-}+1,0\rrbracket
.
One obtains

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}}[e^{-s(L(0,1))}]=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k\rho^{2}(1-\rho)^{k-1}(1+s)^{-(k+1)}=\frac{\rho^{2}}{(\rho+s)^{2}},

which matches the desired expression for

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}}[L(0,1)>h]
.

Analogous calculations give the probabilities under
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}
.
∎

Now we compute the law of the weights on geodesics.

Proof of Proposition
1.5
.

It suffices to compute the law of
L(1,0)\wedge L(0,1)
, under the measure
\mathbf{\Psi}=\frac{(1-\rho)^{2}\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}+\rho^{2}\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}}{(1-\rho)^{2}+\rho^{2}}
.
Note that under either
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}
or
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}
, the random variables
L(1,0)
and
L(0,1)
are independent.
Thus by Lemma
4.2
we get that

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}}[L(1,0)\wedge L(0,1)>h]=(1+\rho h)(1+\rho(1-\rho)h)e^{-h},

and

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}}[L(1,0)\wedge L(0,1)>h]=(1+(1-\rho)h)(1+\rho(1-\rho)h)e^{-h}.

Thus the conclusion follows.
∎

Assuming Theorem
1.1
, we can also compute the proportion of ‘corners’ in geodesics.

Proof of Proposition
1.6
.

Assuming Theorem
1.1
, we have

\frac{N_{n,\rho}}{2n}\to\mathbb{P}_{\nu}[\{(0,0),(0,1),(-1,0)\}\subset\gamma]+\mathbb{P}_{\nu}[\{(0,0),(0,-1),(1,0)\}\subset\gamma],

almost surely as
n\to\infty
.
From the construction of
\nu
, this equals

\frac{(1-\rho)^{2}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}}[L(1,0)>L(0,1)]+\rho^{2}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}}[L(1,0)<L(0,1)]}{(1-\rho)^{2}+\rho^{2}}.

Using that
L(1,0)-L(0,0)
and
L(0,1)-L(0,0)
are independent under either
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}
or
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}
, by Lemma
4.2
we have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}}[L(1,0)>L(0,1)]=\rho^{2}(1+2\rho-2\rho^{2}),\\
&\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}}[L(1,0)<L(0,1)]=(1-\rho)^{2}(1+2\rho-2\rho^{2}).\end{split}

Thus the conclusion follows.
∎

An alternative representation of the weights on geodesics.
We also give an outline of alternative derivation of the
formulae in Proposition
1.5
and Proposition
1.6
,
which also leads to representations of the type mentioned after the statement
of Proposition
1.5
.

Note that under
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}
,
D_{+}
takes values in
\{2,3,\dots\}
and has the distribution of the sum of two independent Geometric random variables
with parameter
1-\rho
. Given
D_{+}
, the random variable
L(1,0)
is the sum of
D_{+}
independent
\Exp(1)
random variables.
From this,
L(1,0)
has the same distribution as
the sum of two
\Exp(1-\rho)
random variables, or equivalently of
\frac{1}{1-\rho}(E_{1}+E_{2})
for
E_{1},E_{2}
i.i.d.
\sim\Exp(1)
.

Meanwhile under
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}
,
D_{-}
takes values in
\{1,2,\dots\}

and has the distribution of the sum of two independent Geometric(
\rho
) random variables minus
1
. Note that if
X\sim\text{Geometric}(\rho)
, then

X-1\overset{d}{=}BX
where
B\sim\text{Bernoulli}(\rho)
independently of
X
. We obtain that
L(0,1)
has the distribution of
\frac{1}{\rho}(E_{3}+BE_{4})
, for

B\sim\text{Bernoulli}(\rho)
and
E_{3},E_{4}
i.i.d.
\sim\Exp(1)
independently of
B
.

Note
L(0,1)
and
L(1,0)
are independent under
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}
.
So we can combine the previous two paragraphs to get that
the distribution of
\xi(\mathbf{0})=L(0,1)\wedge L(1,0)

under
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}
is that of

\frac{1}{1-\rho}(E_{1}+E_{2})\wedge\frac{1}{\rho}(E_{3}+BE_{4}),

for
B\sim\text{Bernoulli}(\rho)
and
(E_{i})_{1\leq 1\leq 4}
i.i.d.
\sim\Exp(1)
independently of
B
.

We continue in the particular case
\rho=1/2
. Then the distribution of
\xi(\mathbf{0})
is the same under
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(1)}
as under
\mathbf{\Psi}^{(2)}
,
and so its distribution under
\mathbf{\Psi}
is again the same, that of

2((E_{1}+E_{2})\wedge(E_{3}+BE_{4}))
for
B\sim\text{Bernoulli}(1/2)
and
(E_{i})_{1\leq 1\leq 4}
i.i.d.
\sim\Exp(1)
independently of
B
.

By elementary arguments involving the memoryless property of exponentials,
this distribution can be seen to be a
(1/4,1/2,1/4)
mixture
of Gamma(
1,1
), Gamma(
2,1
) and Gamma(
3,1
) distributions.

A similar but slightly more involved argument can be made for the case of general
\rho
, to give that the distribution of
\xi(\mathbf{0})
is again a mixture of Gamma(
1,1
), Gamma(
2,1
) and Gamma(
3,1
) distributions,
now with weights

\left(\frac{\rho^{4}+(1-\rho)^{4}}{\rho^{2}+(1-\rho)^{2}},2\rho(1-\rho),\frac{2\rho^{2}(1-\rho)^{2}}{\rho^{2}+(1-\rho)^{2}}\right).

As a function of
\rho\in(0,1)
,
this distribution is stochastically increasing
on
(0,1/2]
, and symmetric around
1/2
.

The path as a competition interface.

As
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
in the i.i.d.
\Exp(1)
random field, the path
\gamma
under
\nu
can also be described as a competition interface.
For
L
under
\mathbf{\Psi}
, by slightly abusing the notations we let
I_{0}:=\{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:L(u)\leq 0\}
and

\xi^{\vee,0}(u):=L(u)\vee 0-L(u-(1,0))\vee L(u-(0,1))\vee 0,

for each
u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
Then like Lemma
3.6
, we can show that
I_{0}
contains the same information as
\zeta_{0}
(whose law under
\mathbf{\Psi}
is explicitly described using
\Psi_{+}
and
\Psi_{-}
above). Namely, we have
(0,0)\in I_{0}
and
(0,1),(1,0)\not\in I_{0}
; and for any
x\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\zeta_{0}(x)=0
if and only if there is some
y\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
(x+y,y)\in I_{0}
and
(x+y,y+1)\not\in I_{0}
, and
\zeta_{0}(x)=1
if and only if there is some
y\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
(x+y-1,y)\in I_{0}
and
(x+y,y)\not\in I_{0}
.

Under
\mathbf{\Psi}
and conditioned on
I_{0}
, the weights
\{\xi^{\vee,0}(u)\}_{u\not\in I_{0}}
are i.i.d.
\Exp(1)
. This is because, for any
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
\xi^{\vee,0}(a,b)
is the waiting time for the particle labeled
b
and the hole labeled
a
to switch, since they are next to each other; and that is i.i.d.
\Exp(1)
for all
(a,b)\not\in I_{0}
, given
\zeta_{0}
.

From
I_{0}
and
\xi^{\vee,0}
under
\mathbf{\Psi}
, we define a competition interface, similar to how the competition interface is defined in Section
3.3
.
Specifically, for any
u\leq v
,
u,v\not\in I_{0}
, let
T^{\vee,0}_{u,v}
and
\Gamma^{\vee,0}_{u,v}
be the passage time and geodesic from
u
to
v
under the weights
\xi^{\vee,0}
.
For any
v\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
, we consider the vertex
u_{*}\not\in I_{0}
with the maximum
T^{\vee,0}_{u_{*},v}
.
If
\Gamma^{\vee,0}_{u_{*},v}
intersects
\mathbb{Z}_{+}\times\{0\}
we let
v\in C_{1}
; otherwise
\Gamma^{\vee,0}_{u_{*},v}
intersects
\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and we let
v\in C_{2}
.
We then have that
(\gamma\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2})+(1/2,1/2)
is the boundary between
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
, using analogues of Lemmas
3.3
and
3.5
.

Using this formulation of
\gamma
and the explicit description of
\zeta_{0}
under
\mathbf{\Psi}
, and passage time estimates (e.g. Theorem
5.2
below) or the convergence of the passage time point-to-line profile to the so-called Airy
2
process (see Theorem
8.7
below), one can possibly show that
\gamma[i]
has transversal fluctuation in the order of
i^{2/3}
for large
i
(here
\gamma[i]
denotes the
i
-th vertex in
\gamma\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
), and even obtain exact formulae for the distribution of its scaling limit. We leave these to future explorations.

5
Geometric estimates for LPP

While so far most arguments are on TASEP and use interacting particle system techniques, for the rest of this paper we will mainly use various LPP geometric arguments.
In this section we do some preparations, by providing some useful tools.
Most results in this section have appeared in the literature.

In this section, we do not fix
\rho\in(0,1)
, and all constants are not assumed to depend on
\rho
, unless otherwise stated.
For
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\rho\in(0,1)
, we denote

\langle a,b\rangle_{\rho}:=\left(\left\lfloor\frac{2(1-\rho)^{2}a}{\rho^{2}+(1-\rho)^{2}}\right\rfloor+b,\left\lceil\frac{2\rho^{2}a}{\rho^{2}+(1-\rho)^{2}}\right\rceil-b\right).

Then recall that
\mathbf{n}^{\rho}=\langle n,0\rangle_{\rho}
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
We also write
\langle 0,b\rangle:=(b,-b)
for any
b\in\mathbb{Z}
.

We start with a basic geometric property called ‘ordering of geodesics’.
Note that for any
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
vertices
u\leq v
, if
u^{\prime}\leq v^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{u,v}
, we must have that
\Gamma_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}
is the part of
\Gamma_{u,v}
between
u^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
(including
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}
).
This immediately leads to the following result.

Lemma 5.1
.

For any
(a_{1},b_{1})
and
(a_{2},b_{2})
, we say
(a_{1},b_{1})\preceq(a_{2},b_{2})
, if
a_{1}\leq a_{2}
and
b_{1}\geq b_{2}
.
For any
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
and
v_{1}
,
v_{2}
such that
u_{1}\leq v_{1}
,
u_{2}\leq v_{2}
and
u_{1}\preceq u_{2}
,
v_{1}\preceq v_{2}
,
and any
w_{1}\in\Gamma_{u_{1},v_{1}}
,
w_{2}\in\Gamma_{u_{2},v_{2}}
, we cannot have
w_{2}\preceq w_{1}
unless
w_{1}=w_{2}
.

We next give estimates on passage times.
We have that
T_{\mathbf{0},(m,n)}
has the same law as the largest eigenvalue of
X^{*}X
where
X
is an
(m+1)\times(n+1)
matrix of i.i.d. standard complex Gaussian entries (see
[
Joh00a
, Proposition 1.4]
).
Hence we get the following one-point estimates from
[
LR10
, Theorem 2]
.

Theorem 5.2
.

There exist constants
c,C>0
, such that for any
m\geq n\geq 1
and
x>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}[T_{\mathbf{0},(m,n)}-(\sqrt{m}+\sqrt{n})^{2}\geq xm^{1/2}n^{-1/6}]\leq Ce^{-cx}.

(5.1)

In addition, for each
\psi>1
, there exist
C^{\prime},c^{\prime}>0
depending on
\psi
such that if
\frac{m}{n}<\psi
, we have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}[T_{\mathbf{0},(m,n)}-(\sqrt{m}+\sqrt{n})^{2}\geq xn^{1/3}]\leq C^{\prime}e^{-c^{\prime}(x^{3/2}\wedge xn^{1/3})},\\
&\mathbb{P}[T_{\mathbf{0},(m,n)}-(\sqrt{m}+\sqrt{n})^{2}\leq-xn^{1/3}]\leq C^{\prime}e^{-c^{\prime}x^{3}},\end{split}

(5.2)

and as a consequence

|\mathbb{E}[T_{\mathbf{0},(m,n)}]-(\sqrt{m}+\sqrt{n})^{2}|\leq C^{\prime}n^{1/3}.

(5.3)

Below we will frequently use parallelograms in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
For simplicity of notations, in the rest of this section, for any vertices
u\leq v
and
x>0
, we let
U_{u,v}^{x}
denote the parallelogram whose one pair of opposite sides have length
2x
, parallel to the anti-diagonal, and have midpoints
u
and
v
respectively.
Formally, we let

U_{u,v}^{x}=\{u+\alpha(v-u)+(y,-y):\alpha\in[0,1],y\in[-x,x]\}.

We next state the following parallelogram estimate.

Proposition 5.3

(
[
BGZ21
, Theorem 4.2]
)
.

Let
U_{1},U_{2}
be the part of
U_{(-m,m),(n,n)}^{n^{2/3}}
below
\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor n/3\rfloor}
and above
\mathbb{L}_{\lceil 2n/3\rceil}
respectively.
For each
\psi<1
, there exist constants
c,C>0
depending only on
\psi
, such that when
|m|<\psi n
,

\mathbb{P}\Big[\sup_{u\in U_{1},v\in U_{2}}|T_{u,v}-\mathbb{E}[T_{u,v}]|\geq xn^{1/3}\Big]\leq Ce^{-c(x^{3/2}\wedge xn^{1/3})}.

Such a result is first proved as
[
BSS14
, Proposition 10.1, 10.5]
, in the setting of Poissonian LPP.
In the setting of exponential LPP a proof is given in
[
BGZ21
, Appendix C]
, following the ideas in
[
BSS14
]
.

We will also need the following estimate on the coalescing probability of two geodesics, for finite and semi-infinite geodesics respectively.

Proposition 5.4

(
[
Zha20
]
)
.

For each
\psi\in(0,1)
, there exists
C>0
, such that

\mathbb{P}[\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\langle n,b_{1}\rangle_{1/2}}\cap\mathbb{L}_{n-m}=\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\langle n,b_{2}\rangle_{1/2}}\cap\mathbb{L}_{n-m}]>1-Cm^{-2/3}|b_{1}-b_{2}|

for any
n,m\in\mathbb{N},b_{1},b_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
, such that
m<n/3
,
|b_{1}|,|b_{2}|<\psi n
.

Proposition 5.5

(
[
BSS19
, Theorem 2]
)
.

For any
\rho\in(0,1)
, there is a constant
C>0
, such that for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
, and
k>1
, we have
\mathbb{P}[\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{\rho}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor r^{3/2}k\rfloor}\neq\Gamma_{\langle 0,r\rangle}^{\rho}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor r^{3/2}k\rfloor}]<Ck^{-2/3}
.

\mathbb{L}_{r}
\mathbf{0}
\langle r,0\rangle_{\rho}
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{\rho}

(a)

For the semi-infinite geodesic
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{\rho}
: Lemma
5.6
stats that with probability
>1-Ce^{-cx^{3}}
, its intersection with
\mathbb{L}_{r}
is within distance
xr^{2/3}
to
\langle r,0\rangle_{\rho}
; Corollary
5.8
stats that with probability
>1-Ce^{-cx^{3}}
, below
\mathbb{L}_{r}
it is contained in
U_{\mathbf{0},\langle r,0\rangle_{\rho}}^{xr^{2/3}}
.

\mathbb{L}_{r}
\mathbf{0}
\langle n,b\rangle_{1/2}
\langle r,b^{\prime}\rangle_{1/2}
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\langle n,b\rangle_{1/2}}

(b)

For the infinite geodesic
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\langle n,b\rangle_{1/2}}
: Corollary
5.9
stats that with probability
>1-Ce^{-cx^{3}}
, below
\mathbb{L}_{r}
it is contained in
U_{\mathbf{0},\langle r,b^{\prime}\rangle_{1/2}}^{xr^{2/3}}
.

Figure 6
:
Illustrations of the transversal fluctuation estimates.

Similar coalescence estimates have also been obtained in various other papers, such as
[
SS20
,
BBS21
]
.

We next give some estimates on transversal fluctuations of geodesics (see Figure
6
).
Such geodesic fluctuation estimates have been proved using various methods in the literature
[
BSS14
,
BG21
,
BGZ21
,
BSS19
,
HS20
,
Zha20
,
BF22
,
EJS21
,
Bha20
]
.
We start with an estimate for semi-infinite geodesics, which is illustrated by Figure
6(a)
.

Lemma 5.6
.

For any
\psi\in(0,1)
, there exist
c,C>0
such that the following is true.
Let
\rho\in(\psi,1-\psi)
, and
r,b_{r}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{\rho}[2r+1]=\langle r,b_{r}\rangle_{\rho}
. Then
\mathbb{P}[|b_{r}|>xr^{2/3}]<Ce^{-cx^{3}}
for any
x>0
.

This bound can be quickly deduced from
[
EJS21
, Theorem 3.1]
or
[
Bha20
, Theorem 2.4]
.
Here we give a proof using the above passage time estimates, and properties of the Busemann function.

Proof of Lemma
5.6
.

In this proof we let
c,C>0
denote small and large constants that depend on
\psi
, and the values can change from line to line.
We assume that
r
and
x
are large enough (depending on
\psi
), since otherwise the conclusion follows obviously.

For simplicity of notations we denote
T_{u,v}^{\bullet}=T_{u,v}-\xi(v)
for any vertices
u\leq v
.
Let
\mathbf{B}
be the Busemann function in direction
\boldsymbol{\rho}
, as defined in Section
3.1
.
By Lemma
3.1
, the event
|b_{r}|>xr^{2/3}
implies that there exists
b\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
|b|>xr^{2/3}
, and
T_{\mathbf{0},\langle r,b\rangle_{\rho}}^{\bullet}+\mathbf{B}(\langle r,b\rangle_{\rho},\mathbf{r}^{\rho})>T_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{r}^{\rho}}^{\bullet}
.
To bound this event, we denote
L_{j}:=\{\langle r,b\rangle_{\rho}:|b-2j\lfloor r^{2/3}\rfloor|\leq r^{2/3}\}
for
j\in\mathbb{Z}
.
For each
j
such that
L_{j}
intersects
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, we have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\big[\max_{u\in L_{j}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}}T_{\mathbf{0},u}^{\bullet}+\mathbf{B}(u,\mathbf{r}^{\rho})>T_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{r}^{\rho}}^{\bullet}\big]\\
<&\mathbb{P}\big[\max_{u\in L_{j}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}}T_{\mathbf{0},u}^{\bullet}-\mathbb{E}[T_{\mathbf{0},u}^{\bullet}]>c_{0}j^{2}r^{1/3}\big]+\mathbb{P}\big[T_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{r}^{\rho}}^{\bullet}-\mathbb{E}[T_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{r}^{\rho}}^{\bullet}]<-c_{0}j^{2}r^{1/3}\big]\\
&+\mathbb{P}\big[\max_{u\in L_{j}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}}\mathbf{B}(u,\mathbf{r}^{\rho})+\mathbb{E}[T_{\mathbf{0},u}^{\bullet}]-\mathbb{E}[T_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{r}^{\rho}}^{\bullet}]>-2c_{0}j^{2}r^{1/3}\big],\end{split}

(5.4)

where
c_{0}>0
is a small constant depending only on
\psi
, and satisfies several conditions to be specified below.
We next show that each term in the right-hand side of (
5.4
) is bounded by
Ce^{-c|j|^{3}}
; then by summing over
j\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
2|j|+1>x
and
L_{j}
intersects
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
(note that the later implies that
|j|\leq 2r(2\lfloor r^{2/3}\rfloor)^{-1}<2r^{1/3}
) we get the conclusion.

•

For the first term, we let
\psi^{\prime}>0
be a small number (depending on
c_{0}
and
\psi
and to be determined). When
L_{j}
is contained in
\{\langle r,b\rangle_{1/2}:|b|<(1-\psi^{\prime})r\}
, we consider the parallelogram
U_{\mathbf{0},\langle r,2j\lfloor r^{2/3}\rfloor\rangle_{\rho}}^{r^{2/3}}
.
Using Proposition
5.3
with this parallelogram we get the desired bound.
When
L_{j}
is not contained in
\{\langle r,b\rangle_{1/2}:|b|<(1-\psi^{\prime})r\}
we cannot directly apply Proposition
5.3
, since the above parallelogram may not satisfy the slope condition there.
Instead, we take some small
\alpha>0
(depending on
c_{0}
and
\psi
and to be determined), and consider the parallelogram
U_{-\langle\lfloor\alpha r\rfloor,0\rangle_{1/2},\langle r,2j\lfloor r^{2/3}\rfloor\rangle_{\rho}}^{r^{2/3}}
.
Using Proposition
5.3
with this parallelogram we get

\mathbb{P}\big[\max_{u\in L_{j}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}}T_{-\langle\lfloor\alpha r\rfloor,0\rangle_{1/2},u}^{\bullet}-\mathbb{E}[T_{-\langle\lfloor\alpha r\rfloor,0\rangle_{1/2},u}^{\bullet}]>2^{-1}c_{0}j^{2}r^{1/3}\big]<Ce^{-c|j|^{3}}.

(5.5)

For any
u\in L_{j}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
we have
T_{\mathbf{0},u}^{\bullet}\leq T_{\langle-\lfloor\alpha r\rfloor,0\rangle_{1/2},u}^{\bullet}
, and

\mathbb{E}[T_{\mathbf{0},u}^{\bullet}]>\mathbb{E}[T_{\langle-\lfloor\alpha r\rfloor,0\rangle_{1/2},u}^{\bullet}]-200^{-1}c_{0}\psi^{2}r>\mathbb{E}[T_{\langle-\lfloor\alpha r\rfloor,0\rangle_{1/2},u}^{\bullet}]-2^{-1}c_{0}j^{2}r^{1/3},

where the two inequalities are due to the following reasons.
The first inequality is by
\mathbb{E}[T_{\mathbf{0},u}^{\bullet}]\geq 2r
and
\mathbb{E}[T_{\langle-\lfloor\alpha r\rfloor,0\rangle_{1/2},u}^{\bullet}]<2r+200^{-1}c_{0}\psi^{2}r
, which is due to (
5.3
) and the fact that
L_{j}
is not contained in
\{\langle r,b\rangle_{1/2}:|b|<(1-\psi^{\prime})r\}
, and taking
\psi^{\prime}
and
\alpha
small enough (depending on
\psi
and
c_{0}
). The second inequality is by
|j|>0.1\psi r^{1/3}
, which is implied by the fact that
L_{j}
is not contained in
\{\langle r,b\rangle_{1/2}:|b|<(1-\psi^{\prime})r\}
.

Thus we have

\max_{u\in L_{j}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}}T_{-\langle\lfloor\alpha r\rfloor,0\rangle_{1/2},u}^{\bullet}-\mathbb{E}[T_{-\langle\lfloor\alpha r\rfloor,0\rangle_{1/2},u}^{\bullet}]>\max_{u\in L_{j}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}}T_{\mathbf{0},u}^{\bullet}-\mathbb{E}[T_{\mathbf{0},u}^{\bullet}]-2^{-1}c_{0}j^{2}r^{1/3},

so the first term in the right-hand side of (
5.4
) is bounded as desired by (
5.5
).

•

For the second term we apply Theorem
5.2
.

•

For the last term, by (
5.1
) and (
5.3
) from Theorem
5.2
, we have
\mathbb{E}[T_{\mathbf{0},\langle r,b\rangle_{\rho}}^{\bullet}]-\mathbb{E}[T_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{r}^{\rho}}^{\bullet}]\leq Cr^{1/3}-b(\rho^{-1}-(1-\rho)^{-1})-c_{1}b^{2}r^{-1}
for any
\langle r,b\rangle_{\rho}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
,
where
c_{1}>0
is determined by
\psi
.
By taking
c_{0}<c_{1}
, and assuming that both
r
and
|j|
are large enough, we have

c_{1}b^{2}r^{-1}-Cr^{1/3}-2c_{0}j^{2}r^{1/3}>c_{0}j^{2}r^{1/3}
when
|b-2j\lfloor r^{2/3}\rfloor|\leq r^{2/3}
.
Then the last term in the right-hand side of (
5.4
) is bounded by

\mathbb{P}\big[\max_{|b-2j\lfloor r^{2/3}\rfloor|\leq r^{2/3}}\mathbf{B}(\langle r,b\rangle_{\rho},\mathbf{r}^{\rho})-b(\rho^{-1}-(1-\rho)^{-1})>c_{0}j^{2}r^{1/3}\big].

Note that
b\mapsto\mathbf{B}(\langle r,b\rangle_{\rho},\mathbf{r}^{\rho})-b(\rho^{-1}-(1-\rho)^{-1})
is a (two-sided) centered random walk, where each step has exponential tail determined by
\rho
(see Section
3.1
).
We can apply concentration inequalities to get the desired bound.

(For example, we can do a Chernoff type estimate as follows. Take any
c_{2}>0
, small enough depending on
\psi
. Without loss of generality we assume
j>0
. Write the random walk as
b\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{b}X_{i}
for
b>0
, where each
X_{i}
has exponential tail determined by
\rho
. Consider
e^{c_{2}jr^{-1/3}\sum_{i=1}^{b}X_{i}}
, which is a supermartingale in
b
.
Let
\tau
be the first time after
(2j-1)\lfloor r^{2/3}\rfloor
when this supermartingale is at least
e^{c_{2}^{3/2}j^{3}}
, or
(2j+1)\lfloor r^{2/3}\rfloor+1
, whichever is smaller.
Then we have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\bigg[\max_{|b-2j\lfloor r^{2/3}\rfloor|\leq r^{2/3}}\sum_{i=1}^{b}X_{i}>c_{2}^{1/2}j^{2}r^{1/3}\bigg]\\
\leq&\mathbb{P}\bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}X_{i}>c_{2}^{1/2}j^{2}r^{1/3}\bigg]=\mathbb{P}[e^{c_{2}jr^{-1/3}\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}X_{i}}>e^{c_{2}^{3/2}j^{3}}]\\
\leq&e^{-c_{2}^{3/2}j^{3}}\mathbb{E}[e^{c_{2}jr^{-1/3}\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}X_{i}}]\\
\leq&e^{-c_{2}^{3/2}j^{3}}\mathbb{E}[e^{c_{2}jr^{-1/3}\sum_{i=1}^{(2j+1)\lfloor r^{2/3}\rfloor+1}X_{i}}]=e^{-c_{2}^{3/2}j^{3}}\mathbb{E}[e^{c_{2}jr^{-1/3}X_{1}}]^{(2j+1)\lfloor r^{2/3}\rfloor+1},\end{split}

and this is bounded by
e^{-c_{2}^{3/2}j^{3}/2}
when
c_{2}
is small enough, since
\mathbb{E}[e^{c_{2}jr^{-1/3}X_{1}}]<e^{Cc_{2}^{2}j^{2}r^{-2/3}}
.)

By these bounds the conclusion follows.
∎

In addition to the above one-point bound, we also quote the following uniform bound on transversal fluctuations of geodesics.

Lemma 5.7

(
[
BGZ21
, Proposition C.9]
)
.

For each
\psi\in(0,1)
there exist constants
c,C>0
such that the following is true.
For
x>0
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
|b|<\psi n
, the probability that the geodesic
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\langle n,b\rangle_{1/2}}
exits
U_{\mathbf{0},\langle n,b\rangle_{1/2}}^{xn^{2/3}}
is at most
Ce^{-cx^{3}}
.

The following result for semi-infinite geodesics follows immediately by combining Lemma
5.6
and Lemma
5.7
. See also Figure
6(a)
.

Corollary 5.8
.

For each
\psi\in(0,1)
, there exist constants
c,C>0
such that the following is true.
Take any
r\in\mathbb{N}
large enough, any
x>0
, and any
\rho\in(\psi,1-\psi)
.
Then with probability at least
1-Ce^{-cx^{3}}
, the part of the geodesic
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{\rho}
below
\mathbb{L}_{r}
is contained in
U_{\mathbf{0},\langle r,0\rangle_{\rho}}^{xr^{2/3}}
.

We also have such near-end transversal fluctuation estimate for finite geodesics (see Figure
6(b)
).

Corollary 5.9
.

For each
\psi\in(0,1)
, there exist constants
c,C>0
such that the following is true.
Take any integers
0<r<n
that are large enough, any
x>0
, and any integer
b
with
|b|<\psi n
.
Let
\langle r,b^{\prime}\rangle_{1/2}
be the vertex in
\mathbb{L}_{r}
that is closest to the straight line connecting
\mathbf{0}
and
\langle n,b\rangle_{1/2}
.
Then with probability at least
1-Ce^{-cx^{3}}
, the geodesic
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\langle n,b\rangle_{1/2}}
below
\mathbb{L}_{r}
is contained in
U_{\mathbf{0},\langle r,b^{\prime}\rangle_{1/2}}^{xr^{2/3}}
.

Proof.

Let
c,C>0
denote small and large constants depending only on
\psi
, and the values can change from line to line.
When
x>2r^{1/3}
the conclusion follows obviously, so we can assume that
x\leq 2r^{1/3}
.
We now take
\rho_{-},\rho_{+}\in(0,1)
, such that
\mathbf{n}^{\rho_{-}}=\langle n,b-\lfloor cxn^{1/3}\rfloor\rangle_{1/2}
and
\mathbf{n}^{\rho_{+}}=\langle n,b+\lfloor cxn^{1/3}\rfloor\rangle_{1/2}
.
Take
b_{-},b_{+}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\langle n,b_{-}\rangle_{1/2}\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{\rho_{-}}
and
\langle n,b_{+}\rangle_{1/2}\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{\rho_{+}}
.
By Lemma
5.6
, with probability at least
1-Ce^{-cx^{3}}
we have
b_{-}<b<b_{+}
, thus
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\langle n,b\rangle_{1/2}}
is sandwiched between
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{\rho_{-}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{\rho_{+}}
below
\mathbb{L}_{r}
by ordering of geodesics (Lemma
5.1
).
By Corollary
5.8
, with probability at least
1-Ce^{-cx^{3}}
,
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{\rho_{-}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{\rho_{+}}
below
\mathbb{L}_{r}
are both contained in
U_{\mathbf{0},\langle r,b^{\prime}\rangle_{1/2}}^{xr^{2/3}}
, so the conclusion follows.
∎

Finally, we have the following estimate on the passage time along a semi-infinite geodesic.

\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor cl\rfloor}
\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor Cl\rfloor}
\mathbb{H}_{l}^{\rho}
\mathbf{0}
u_{*}
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{\rho}

Figure 7
:
An illustration of Lemma
5.10
and its proof: the yellow region is the parallelogram
U=U_{\langle\lfloor cl\rfloor,0\rangle_{\rho},\langle\lfloor Cl\rfloor,0\rangle_{\rho}}^{cx^{1/2}l^{2/3}}
, and
V
is the set of vertices within distance
1
from the green segment. When
c
is small and
C
is large (depending on
\rho
), if
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{\rho}
between
\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor cl\rfloor}
and
\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor Cl\rfloor}
is contained in
U
, it must intersect the line
\mathbb{H}_{l}^{\rho}
inside
U
.

For simplicity of notations, below we denote (recall that
\boldsymbol{\rho}=((1-\rho)^{2},\rho^{2})
)

\mathbb{H}_{x}^{\rho}:=\{x\boldsymbol{\rho}+y((1-\rho),-\rho):y\in\mathbb{R}\},

for any
\rho\in(0,1)
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Note that
\mathbb{H}_{x}
intersect the axes at
(0,x\rho)
and
(x(1-\rho),0)
.

Lemma 5.10
.

For each
\psi\in(0,1)
, there exist constants
c,C>0
such that the following is true.
Take any
\rho\in(\psi,1-\psi)
and
l>0
.
Let
u_{*}
be the first vertex in
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{\rho}
above the line
\mathbb{H}_{l}^{\rho}
.
Then
\mathbb{P}[|T_{\mathbf{0},u_{*}}-l|>xl^{1/3}]<Ce^{-cx}
for any
0<x<cl^{2/3}
.

Proof.

Let
c,C>0
denote small and large constants depending only on
\psi
, and below the values can change from line to line.
Let
U=U_{\langle\lfloor cl\rfloor,0\rangle_{\rho},\langle\lfloor Cl\rfloor,0\rangle_{\rho}}^{cx^{1/2}l^{2/3}}
.
Let
V
be the set of all
v\in U
that is within distance
1
to the line
\mathbb{H}_{l}^{\rho}
.
By Corollary
5.8
, with probability at least
1-Ce^{-cx^{3/2}}
, the geodesic
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{\rho}
between
\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor cl\rfloor}
and
\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor Cl\rfloor}
is contained in
U
, thus
u_{*}\in U
and
u_{*}\in V
, since (when
c
is small and
C
is large) we must have that
\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor cl\rfloor}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
is below
\mathbb{H}_{l}^{\rho}
and
\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor Cl\rfloor}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
is above
\mathbb{H}_{l}^{\rho}
(see Figure
7
).

By (
5.3
) in Theorem
5.2
, we have
|\mathbb{E}[T_{\mathbf{0},v}]-l|<cxl^{1/3}
for any
v\in V
.
It remains to show that

\mathbb{P}\bigg[\max_{v\in V}|T_{\mathbf{0},v}-\mathbb{E}[T_{\mathbf{0},v}]|>cxl^{1/3}\bigg]<Ce^{-cx}.

(5.6)

For this, we split
V
into
\lceil x^{1/2}\rceil
sets
V_{1},\ldots,V_{\lceil x^{1/2}\rceil}
, each with diameter
<cl^{2/3}
. Since
cx^{1/2}l^{2/3}<cl
, the slope of any line passing through
\mathbf{0}
and intersecting
V
is bounded away from
0
and
\infty
. So we can apply Proposition
5.3
to each
V_{i}
and conclude that

\mathbb{P}\bigg[\max_{v\in V_{i}}|T_{\mathbf{0},v}-\mathbb{E}[T_{\mathbf{0},v}]|>cxl^{1/3}\bigg]<Ce^{-c(x^{3/2}\wedge xl^{2/3})}.

By a union bound over
i
we get (
5.6
), so the conclusion follows.
∎

Combining Corollary
5.8
and Lemma
5.10
we get the following (see Figure
8
).

Corollary 5.11
.

For each
\psi\in(0,1)
, there exist constants
c,C>0
such that the following is true.
Take any
\rho\in(\psi,1-\psi)
and
l>0
, and let
u_{*}
be the last vertex in
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{\rho}
with
T_{\mathbf{0},u_{*}}\leq l
.
Then for any
0<x<cl^{2/9}
, with probability
>1-Ce^{-cx^{3}}
the vertex
u_{*}
is between the lines
\mathbb{H}_{l-x^{3}l^{1/3}}^{\rho}
and
\mathbb{H}_{l+x^{3}l^{1/3}}^{\rho}
, and
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},u_{*}}\subset U_{\mathbf{0},\langle l,0\rangle_{\rho}}^{xl^{2/3}}
.

\mathbb{H}_{l-x^{3}l^{1/3}}^{\rho}
\mathbb{H}_{l+x^{3}l^{1/3}}^{\rho}
\mathbb{L}_{l}
\mathbf{0}
u_{*}
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{\rho}

Figure 8
:
An illustration of Corollary
5.11
: the yellow region is the parallelogram
U_{\mathbf{0},\langle l,0\rangle_{\rho}}^{xl^{2/3}}
. When
x<cl^{2/9}
, the intersections between
U_{\mathbf{0},\langle l,0\rangle_{\rho}}^{xl^{2/3}}
and the lines
\mathbb{H}_{l-x^{3}l^{1/3}}^{\rho}
and
\mathbb{H}_{l+x^{3}l^{1/3}}^{\rho}
are strictly below
\mathbb{L}_{l}
. Thus if
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{\rho}
below
\mathbb{L}_{l}
is contained in
U_{\mathbf{0},\langle l,0\rangle_{\rho}}^{xl^{2/3}}
, the part of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{\rho}
between
\mathbb{H}_{l-x^{3}l^{1/3}}^{\rho}
and
\mathbb{H}_{l+x^{3}l^{1/3}}^{\rho}
must also be contained in
U_{\mathbf{0},\langle l,0\rangle_{\rho}}^{xl^{2/3}}
; and if in addition
u_{*}
is between
\mathbb{H}_{l-x^{3}l^{1/3}}^{\rho}
and
\mathbb{H}_{l+x^{3}l^{1/3}}^{\rho}
, we must have
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},u_{*}}\subset U_{\mathbf{0},\langle l,0\rangle_{\rho}}^{xl^{2/3}}
.

6
Convergence of TASEP as seen from an isolated second-class particle

Starting from this section, we again always fix
\rho\in(0,1)
, and the choice of all other parameters and constants can depend on
\rho
unless otherwise stated.

Using geometric arguments and estimates from Section
5
, in this section we upgrade Proposition
2.7
to Theorem
1.7
.
The general idea is to show that
\Phi_{t}
and
\Phi_{t+s}
are close when
s
is much smaller than
t
.

Proposition 6.1
.

For any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a constant
C>0
such that the following is true.
Take any
s,t>C
with
t<s^{1.01}
, and any continuous function
f:\{0,1,*\}^{\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket}\to[0,1]
, regarded as a function on
\{0,1,*\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, we have

|\Phi_{t}(f)-\Phi_{t+s}(f)|<C(s/t)^{1/30}
.

Using this we can deduce Theorem
1.7
.

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

Take any
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
f:\{0,1,*\}^{\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket}\to[0,1]
, regarded as a function on
\{0,1,*\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, it suffices to show that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\Phi_{t}(f)=\Psi(f).

(6.1)

Take any
\delta>0
.
By Proposition
2.7
we have that
(\delta t)^{-1}\int_{0}^{\delta t}\Phi_{t+s}(f)ds\to\Psi(f)
as
t\to\infty
.
By Proposition
6.1
we have for any
t>C
,

\left|\Phi_{t}(f)-(\delta t)^{-1}\int_{0}^{\delta t}\Phi_{t+s}(f)ds\right|\leq(\delta t)^{-1}\int_{t^{1/1.01}}^{\delta t}|\Phi_{t}(f)-\Phi_{t+s}(f)|ds+(\delta t)^{-1}t^{1/1.01}\\
<C\delta^{1/30}+(\delta t)^{-1}t^{1/1.01},

where
C
is a constant depending on
N
.
Thus
\limsup_{t\to\infty}|\Phi_{t}(f)-\Psi(f)|\leq C\delta^{1/30}
, and by sending
\delta\to 0
we get (
6.1
).
∎

To prove Proposition
6.1
, we construct a coupling between
\Phi_{t}
and
\Phi_{t+s}
.
For this we recall the setup of TASEP as seen from a hole-particle pair (or equivalently a second-class particle).

Let
(\eta_{t}^{-})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\eta_{t}^{+})_{t\geq 0}
be two copies of TASEP, with
\eta_{0}^{-}(0)=\eta_{0}^{+}(0)=0
and
\eta_{0}^{-}(1)=\eta_{0}^{+}(1)=1
; and all
\eta_{0}^{-}(x),\eta_{0}^{+}(x)
for
x\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0,1\}
are i.i.d. Bernoulli
(\rho)
.
In both copies, we label the holes by
\mathbb{Z}
from left to right, with the hole at site
0
at time
0
labeled
0
;
and label the particles by
\mathbb{Z}
from right to left, with the particle at site
1
at time
0
labeled
0
.
Keeping track of the hole-particle pair starting from sites
0
and
1
, as described in Section
3.3
, we denote
p_{t}^{-}=(a_{t}^{-},b_{t}^{-})
and
p_{t}^{+}=(a_{t}^{+},b_{t}^{+})
as the labels of the tracked hole and particle at time
t
(in
(\eta_{t}^{-})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\eta_{t}^{+})_{t\geq 0}
respectively).

For notational convenience, we also denote
\hat{\eta}^{-}_{t}=\eta^{-}_{t}(x+a_{t}^{-}-b_{t}^{-}+\cdot)
,
\hat{\eta}^{+}_{t}=\eta^{+}_{t}(x+a_{t}^{+}-b_{t}^{+}+\cdot)
for any
t\geq 0
.
Then
(\hat{\eta}_{t}^{-})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\hat{\eta}_{t}^{+})_{t\geq 0}
are TASEPs as seen from a hole-particle pair, and by replacing the hole-particle pair in
\hat{\eta}_{t}^{-}
or
\hat{\eta}_{t}^{+}
by a second-class particle we get the distribution
\Phi_{t}
(defined in Section
2.2
).

Below we fix
s>0
.
Our general strategy to couple the processes
(\hat{\eta}_{t}^{-})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\hat{\eta}_{t+s}^{+})_{t\geq 0}
so that they evolve with the same set of waiting times (to be explained shortly).
We implement this via coupling TASEP and LPP as described at the beginning of Section
3.3
, and coupling the corresponding LPP models.
For this, let’s set up some useful notations.

•

For any
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
, if in
{\eta_{0}^{-}}
(resp.
{\eta_{0}^{+}}
) the particle with label
b
is to the left of the hole with label
a
, we denote
L^{-}(a,b)
(resp.
L^{+}(a,b)
) as the time when they switch; otherwise we set
L^{-}(a,b)=0
(resp.
L^{+}(a,b)=0
).
Let
\{\xi^{-}(u)\}_{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
(resp.
\{\xi^{+}(u)\}_{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
) be i.i.d.
\Exp(1)
weights, and below we work under the almost sure event that there is a unique geodesic between any
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
vertices
u\leq v
under these weights, and from any
u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
there is a unique semi-infinite geodesic in direction
\boldsymbol{\rho}
under these random weights, and all these semi-infinite geodesics coalesce.
We let
\mathbf{G}^{-}
(resp.
\mathbf{G}^{+}
) be the LPP Busemann function in direction
\boldsymbol{\rho}
under these random weights (defined like
\mathbf{G}
in Section
3.1
).
We couple
(\eta_{t}^{-})_{t\geq 0}
(resp.
(\eta_{t}^{+})_{t\geq 0}
) with
\{\xi^{-}(u)\}_{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
(resp.
\{\xi^{+}(u)\}_{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
) so that
\mathbf{G}^{-}\vee 0=L^{-}
(resp.
\mathbf{G}^{+}\vee 0=L^{+}
) almost surely, and below we work under the event that this equality holds.

•

We use
I_{t}^{-}
,
\xi^{-,\vee}
,
\xi^{-,\vee,t}
,
T_{u,v}^{-}
,
\Gamma_{u,v}^{-}
,
\Gamma_{u}^{-}
,
\Gamma_{u}^{-,\vee}
(resp.
I_{t}^{+}
,
\xi^{+,\vee}
,
\xi^{+,\vee,t}
,
T_{u,v}^{+}
,
\Gamma_{u,v}^{+}
,
\Gamma_{u}^{+}
,
\Gamma_{u}^{+,\vee}
) to denote the objects
I_{t}
(growth process),
\xi^{\vee}
(dual weights),
\xi^{\vee,t}
(dual weights from
I_{t}
),
T_{u,v}
(passage time),
\Gamma_{u,v}
(finite geodesic),
\Gamma_{u}
(semi-infinite geodesic),
\Gamma_{u}^{\vee}
(downward semi-infinite geodesic) defined in the introduction, and Section
3.1
, Section
3.2
, under the weights
\xi^{-}
(resp.
\xi^{+}
).
We shall also work under the almost sure event that these downward semi-infinite geodesics (under these weights) exist and are uniqueness and coalescence.
In addition, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
we let

\begin{split}&\partial I_{t}^{+}:=\{u\in I_{t}^{+}:\mathbf{G}^{+}(u+(1,0))\vee\mathbf{G}^{+}(u+(0,1))>t\},\\
&\partial I_{t}^{-}:=\{u\in I_{t}^{-}:\mathbf{G}^{-}(u+(1,0))\vee\mathbf{G}^{-}(u+(0,1))>t\}.\end{split}

•

For
t\geq 0
,

p_{t}^{-}=(a_{t}^{-},b_{t}^{-})
(resp.
p_{t}^{+}=(a_{t}^{+},b_{t}^{+})
) is the last vertex in
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{-}\cap I_{t}^{-}
(resp.
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{+}\cap I_{t}^{+}
), by Lemma
3.7
.

We first describe the coupling between
\hat{\eta}_{0}^{-}
and
\hat{\eta}_{s}^{+}
.
Take
r\in\mathbb{N}
. For any coupling between
\hat{\eta}_{0}^{-}
and
\hat{\eta}_{s}^{+}
, we denote
\mathcal{A}
as the event where

\hat{\eta}_{0}^{-}(x)=\hat{\eta}_{s}^{+}(x),\forall x\in\mathbb{Z},|x|>r;\quad\sum_{x=-r}^{r}\hat{\eta}_{0}^{-}(x)=\sum_{x=-r}^{r}\hat{\eta}_{s}^{+}(x).

By Lemma
3.6
, under
\mathcal{A}
we can find a (unique)
p^{*}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, such that
I_{0}^{-}\cap\{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:|ad(u)|>r\}=(I_{s}^{+}-p^{*})\cap\{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:|ad(u)|>r\}
, and
ad(p^{*})=ad(p_{s}^{+})
.
In particular, this implies that

I_{0}^{-}\setminus\{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:|ad(u)|,|d(u)|\leq r\}=(I_{s}^{+}-p^{*})\setminus\{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:|ad(u)|,|d(u)|\leq r\}.

(6.2)

Lemma 6.2
.

There is a coupling of
\hat{\eta}_{0}^{-}
and
\hat{\eta}_{s}^{+}
such that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}]>1-C(rs^{-2/3})^{-1/10}
when
Cs^{2/3}<r<s^{2/3+0.01}
and
s>C
, where
C>0
is a constant.

We leave the construction of this coupling to the next subsection, and proceed to couple
(\hat{\eta}_{t}^{-})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\hat{\eta}_{s+t}^{+})_{t\geq 0}
.
The idea is actually straightforward: we just couple the exponential waiting times. Namely, we note that for any
(a,b)\not\in I_{0}^{-}
,
\xi^{-,\vee,0}(a,b)
is precisely the waiting time for the hole labeled
a
to switch with the particle labeled
b
since they are next to each other; and conditioned on
I_{0}^{-}
,
\{\xi^{-,\vee,0}(u)\}_{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus I_{0}^{-}}
are i.i.d.
\Exp(1)
(see also Lemma
3.4
). The same is true for
\{\xi^{+,\vee,s}(u)\}_{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus I_{s}^{+}}
conditioned on
I_{s}^{+}
.
So we just couple these two sets of waiting times (as much as possible), up to a translation by
p^{*}
.

We note that, for
(\hat{\eta}_{t}^{-})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\hat{\eta}_{s+t}^{+})_{t\geq 0}
, we need to couple them conditioned on
\hat{\eta}_{0}^{-}
and
\hat{\eta}_{s}^{+}
.
We next show that, under
\mathcal{A}
,
\hat{\eta}_{0}^{-}
and
\hat{\eta}_{s}^{+}
and
p^{*}
contain precisely the same information as
I_{0}^{-}
and
I_{s}^{+}
. (Then conditioned on
\hat{\eta}_{0}^{-}
and
\hat{\eta}_{s}^{+}
and
p^{*}
, the waiting times
\{\xi^{-,\vee,0}(u)\}_{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus I_{0}^{-}}
and
\{\xi^{+,\vee,s}(u)\}_{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus I_{s}^{+}}
are i.i.d.
\Exp(1)
.)
Indeed,
\hat{\eta}_{0}^{-}
is just
\eta_{0}^{-}
, which determines
I_{0}^{-}
according to Lemma
3.6
.
Using Lemma
3.6
we also get that
\hat{\eta}_{s}^{+}
determines
I_{s}^{+}
, up to a translation of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
; and the translation can be uniquely determined using
p^{*}
and the fact that
I_{0}^{-}
and
I_{s}^{+}-p^{*}
are the same outside a compact set.
In the other direction, given
I_{s}^{+}
and
I_{0}^{-}
we can uniquely find
p^{*}
, and (by Lemma
3.6
)
\eta_{0}^{-}=\hat{\eta}_{0}^{-}
is determined by
I_{0}^{-}
, and
\eta_{s}^{+}
is determined by
I_{s}^{+}
. To get
\hat{\eta}_{s}^{+}
we just shift
\eta_{s}^{+}
by
ad(p^{*})
.

We now couple
(\hat{\eta}_{t}^{-})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\hat{\eta}_{s+t}^{+})_{t\geq 0}
. We let
\hat{\eta}_{0}^{-}
and
\hat{\eta}_{s}^{+}
be coupled using the coupling from Lemma
6.2
. If
\mathcal{A}
does not hold, we just couple
(\hat{\eta}_{t}^{-})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\hat{\eta}_{s+t}^{+})_{t\geq 0}
(conditioned on
\hat{\eta}_{0}^{-}
and
\hat{\eta}_{s}^{+}
) arbitrarily.
If
\mathcal{A}
holds, we couple
(\hat{\eta}_{t}^{-})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\hat{\eta}_{s+t}^{+})_{t\geq 0}
(conditioned on
\hat{\eta}_{0}^{-}
and
\hat{\eta}_{s}^{+}
and
p^{*}
) so that the event

\xi^{-,\vee,0}(u)=\xi^{+,\vee,s}(u+p^{*}),\quad\forall u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus(I_{0}^{-}\cup(I_{s}^{+}-p^{*}))

(6.3)

holds with probability
1
. Below we work under this event whenever
\mathcal{A}
holds.

We bound the total variation distance between the
\{0,1,*\}^{\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket}
marginals of
\Phi_{t}
and
\Phi_{t+s}
(thus prove Proposition
6.1
), by bounding the probability that
\hat{\eta}_{t}^{-}
and
\hat{\eta}_{t+s}^{+}
are different in
\llbracket-N,N+1\rrbracket
under this coupling.

In the LPP setting, this is to show that with high probability, for any
u
around
p^{-}_{t}
we have
L^{-}(u)=L^{+}(u+p^{*})-s
.
By Lemma
3.5
, this is implied by that, for such
u
the paths
\Gamma^{-,\vee}_{u}\setminus I_{0}^{-}
and
\Gamma^{+,\vee}_{u+p^{*}}\setminus I_{s}^{-}-p^{*}
are the same, and is contained in the area
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus(I_{0}^{-}\cup(I_{s}^{+}-p^{*}))
where the weights are coupled.
Using the non-crossing property (Lemma
3.3
), this is ensured by coalescence of (upward) semi-finite geodesics.
More precisely we consider the following events (which are also illustrated in Figure
9
).

Take
m,r\in\mathbb{N}
with
r<m
.

•

Let
\mathcal{B}_{-}
be the event where

\exists u_{-,1},u_{-,2}\in\partial I_{0}^{-},\quad ad(u_{-,1})<-r,ad(u_{-,2})>r,\;d(u_{-,1}),d(u_{-,2})<2m,\\
\Gamma_{u_{-,1}}^{-}\cap\mathbb{L}_{m}=\Gamma_{u_{-,2}}^{-}\cap\mathbb{L}_{m}.

•

Let
\mathcal{B}_{+}
be the event where
\mathcal{A}
happens (with the same
r
), and in addition

\exists u_{+,1},u_{+,2}\in\partial I_{s}^{+},\quad ad(u_{+,1}-p^{*})<-r,\;ad(u_{+,2}-p^{*})>r,\;d(u_{+,1}-p*),d(u_{+,2}-p*)<2m,\\
(\Gamma_{u_{+,1}}^{-}-p^{*})\cap\mathbb{L}_{m}=(\Gamma_{u_{+,2}}^{-}-p^{*})\cap\mathbb{L}_{m}.

•

For any
t>0
we let
\mathcal{F}_{t}
be the event where
d(p_{t}^{-})>2m+2N+2
.

Lemma 6.3
.

For any
t>0
, under
\mathcal{B}_{-}\cap\mathcal{B}_{+}\cap\mathcal{F}_{t}
we have that
\hat{\eta}_{t}^{-}
equals
\hat{\eta}_{t+s}^{+}
in
\llbracket-N,N+1\rrbracket
.

To prove this, we mainly need to establish the following result.

Lemma 6.4
.

Under
\mathcal{B}_{-}\cap\mathcal{B}_{+}
, we have

1.

L^{-}(u)=L^{+}(u+p^{*})-s
for any
u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with
d(u)>2m
,
u\not\in I_{0}^{-}
,
u+p^{*}\not\in I_{s}^{+}
.

2.

p_{t}^{-}=p_{t+s}^{+}-p^{*}
for any
t>0
with
d(p_{t}^{-})\geq 2m
.

\mathbf{0}
u_{+,2}
u_{+,1}
\mathbf{0}
u_{-,1}
u_{-,2}
\mathbb{L}_{m}
I_{s}^{+}
I_{0}^{-}

Figure 9
:
An illustration of the events
\mathcal{B}_{-}
and
\mathcal{B}_{+}
, translated and superposed together.
The red objects are for
\mathcal{B}_{-}
, and are constructed from
({\eta_{t}^{-}})_{t\geq 0}
; and the blue objects are for
\mathcal{B}_{+}
and are constructed from
({\eta_{t}^{+}})_{t\geq 0}
. The difference between the red
\mathbf{0}
and blue
\mathbf{0}
is
p^{*}
.

Proof.

Since
r<m
, under
\mathcal{A}
we have
\{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:d(u)>2m\}\cap I_{0}^{-}=\{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:d(u)>2m\}\cap(I_{s}^{+}-p^{*})
by (
6.2
).
Denote
U=\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus(I_{0}^{-}\cup(I_{s}^{+}-p^{*}))
.

We first show that, under
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}_{-}
, we must have
\Gamma_{u}^{-,\vee}\setminus I_{0}^{-}\subset U
for any
u\in U
with
d(u)>2m
.
Indeed, by the non-crossing property (Lemma
3.3
), the path
\Gamma_{u_{-,1}}^{-}+(1/2,1/2)
divides
u_{-,1}+(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}^{2})
into two parts
P_{1,\downarrow}
(the lower-right part) and
P_{1,\uparrow}
(the upper-left part) such that
\Gamma_{u}^{-,\vee}
intersects at most one of them, and the path
\Gamma_{u_{-,2}}^{-}+(1/2,1/2)
divides
u_{-,2}+(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}^{2})
into two parts
P_{2,\downarrow}
(the lower-right part) and
P_{2,\uparrow}
(the upper-left part) such that
\Gamma_{u}^{-,\vee}
intersects at most one of them.
Since
\Gamma_{u_{-,1}}^{-}\cap\mathbb{L}_{m}=\Gamma_{u_{-,2}}^{-}\cap\mathbb{L}_{m}
, we must have

\{u:d(u)>2m\}\cap P_{1,\downarrow}=\{u:d(u)>2m\}\cap P_{2,\downarrow},\;\{u:d(u)>2m\}\cap P_{1,\uparrow}=\{u:d(u)>2m\}\cap P_{2,\uparrow}.

For any
u\in U
with
d(u)>2m
, depending on whether
u\in P_{1,\uparrow},P_{2,\uparrow}
or
u\in P_{1,\downarrow},P_{2,\downarrow}
, we must have that
\Gamma_{u}^{-,\vee}\setminus I_{0}^{-}\subset P_{1,\uparrow}
or
\Gamma_{u}^{-,\vee}\setminus I_{0}^{-}\subset P_{2,\downarrow}
.
Denote the lower endpoint of
\Gamma_{u}^{-,\vee}\setminus I_{0}^{-}
as
v_{0}
.
By Lemma
3.5
, we have
v_{0}-(0,1),v_{0}-(1,0)\in I_{0}^{-}
, so
v_{0}\in u_{-,1}+\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}
(if
v_{0}\in P_{1,\uparrow}
) or
v_{0}\in u_{-,2}+\mathbb{Z}_{+}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
(if
v_{0}\in P_{2,\downarrow}
).
Thus we have
v_{0}\not\in I_{s}^{+}-p^{*}
, by (
6.2
) and the fact that
ad(u_{-,1})<-r
,
ad(u_{-,2})>r
.
So
v_{0}\in U
, which implies that
\Gamma_{u}^{-,\vee}\setminus I_{0}^{-}\subset U
.

Similarly, under
\mathcal{B}_{+}
we have that
\Gamma_{u+p^{*}}^{+,\vee}\setminus I_{s}^{+}-p^{*}\subset U
for any
u\in U
with
d(u)>2m
.

According to (
6.3
) we have
\xi^{-,\vee,0}(u)=\xi^{+,\vee,s}(u+p^{*})
for any
u\in U
.
Then by Lemma
3.5
, for any
u\in U
with
d(u)>2m
, since we have shown that
\Gamma_{u}^{-,\vee}\setminus I_{0}^{-}\subset U
and
\Gamma_{u+p^{*}}^{+,\vee}\setminus I_{s}^{+}-p^{*}\subset U
, we must have that

\Gamma_{u}^{-,\vee}\setminus I_{0}^{-}=\Gamma_{u+p^{*}}^{+,\vee}\setminus I_{s}^{+}-p^{*},

(6.4)

and
L^{-}(u)=L^{+}(u+p^{*})-s
.
Thus the first statement holds.

We next prove the second statement. Below we always assume
\mathcal{B}_{-}\cap\mathcal{B}_{+}
.
Using that
p_{t}^{-}
is the last vertex in
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{-}\cap I_{t}^{-}
and
p_{t+s}^{+}
is the last vertex in
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{+}\cap I_{t+s}^{+}
, and the first statement,
it suffices to show that

\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{-}\cap\{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:d(u)>2m\}+p^{*}=\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{+}\cap\{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:d(u)>2m+d(p^{*})\}.

(6.5)

By the non-crossing property (Lemma
3.3
),
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{-}\cap\{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:d(u)>2m\}
is determined by
\Gamma_{u}^{-,\vee}
for all
u\in U
with
d(u)\geq 2m
.
More precisely, for all
u\in U
with
d(u)>2m
, we divide them into two parts, depending on whether the lower endpoint of
\Gamma_{u}^{-,\vee}\setminus I_{0}^{-}
is in
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}
or
\mathbb{Z}_{+}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
, and
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{-}\cap\{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:d(u)\geq 2m\}+(1/2,1/2)
is the boundary of these two parts.
Similarly, we can also divide all
u\in U
with
d(u)>2m
into two parts, depending on whether the lower endpoint of
\Gamma_{u+p^{*}}^{+,\vee}\setminus I_{s}^{+}
is in
p_{s}^{+}+\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}
or
p_{s}^{+}+\mathbb{Z}_{+}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
, and
(\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{+}-p^{*})\cap\{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:d(u)\geq 2m\}+(1/2,1/2)
is the boundary of these two parts.

To prove (
6.5
), it then remains to show that, for any
u\in U
with
d(u)>2m
,

\begin{split}&\text{the lower endpoint of }\Gamma_{u}^{-,\vee}\setminus I_{0}^{-}\text{ is in }\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}\\
&\text{if and only if }\\
&\text{the lower endpoint of }\Gamma_{u+p^{*}}^{+,\vee}\setminus I_{s}^{+}\text{ is in }p_{s}^{+}+\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}.\end{split}

(6.6)

For this, we denote the lower endpoint of
\Gamma_{u}^{-,\vee}\setminus I_{0}^{-}
as
v_{0}
.
By (
6.4
),
v_{0}+p^{*}
is the lower endpoint of
\Gamma_{u+p^{*}}^{+,\vee}\setminus I_{s}^{+}
.
Recall that we have
ad(p^{+}_{s}-p^{*})=0
, so
v_{0}
is either to in both
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
p^{+}_{s}-p^{*}+\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, or in both
\mathbb{Z}_{+}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
and
p^{+}_{s}-p^{*}+\mathbb{Z}_{+}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
.
Thus we get (
6.6
), which implies (
6.5
) and that the conclusion follows.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.3
.

The event that
\hat{\eta}_{t}^{-}
equals
\hat{\eta}_{t+s}^{+}
in
\llbracket-N,N+1\rrbracket
can be written as

\{\eta_{t}^{-}(x+ad(p_{t}^{-}))\}_{x\in\llbracket-N,N+1\rrbracket}=\{\eta_{t+s}^{+}(x+ad(p_{t+s}^{+}))\}_{x\in\llbracket-N,N+1\rrbracket}.

By Lemma
3.6
, this is implied by

(I_{t}^{-}-p_{t}^{-})\cap\llbracket-N-1,N+1\rrbracket^{2}=(I_{t+s}^{+}-p_{t+s}^{+})\cap\llbracket-N-1,N+1\rrbracket^{2}.

(6.7)

Below we assume
\mathcal{B}_{-}\cap\mathcal{B}_{+}\cap\mathcal{F}_{t}
. For any
u\in\llbracket-N-1,N+1\rrbracket^{2}+p_{t}^{-}
, we have
d(u)>2m
by
\mathcal{F}_{t}
.
Since under
\mathcal{A}
the sets
I_{0}^{-}
and
I_{s}^{+}-p^{*}
are the same outside
\{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:|ad(u)|,|d(u)|\leq r\}
(as stated by (
6.2
)), and
m>r
, we have either
u\in I_{0}^{-}\cap(I_{s}^{+}-p^{*})
or
u\not\in I_{0}^{-}\cup(I_{s}^{+}-p^{*})
.
In the later case we have
L^{-}(u)=L^{+}(u+p^{*})-s
, by the first statement of Lemma
6.4
.
Thus we always has that either
u\in I_{t}^{-}\cap(I_{t+s}^{+}-p^{*})
, or
u\not\in I_{t}^{-}\cup(I_{t+s}^{+}-p^{*})
.
Thus we have

(\llbracket-N-1,N+1\rrbracket^{2}+p_{t}^{-})\cap I_{t}^{-}=(\llbracket-N-1,N+1\rrbracket^{2}+p_{t}^{-})\cap(I_{t+s}^{+}-p^{*}).

By the second statement of Lemma
6.4
we have
p_{t}^{-}=p_{t+s}^{+}-p^{*}
, so we get (
6.7
), and the conclusion follows.
∎

To prove Proposition

, it remains to lower bound

,

For this we set up some additional notations.

we denote

and
\mathbb{H}_{A}:=\cup_{x\in A}\mathbb{H}_{x}
.

:
