, we denote
\mathcal{M}_{\lambda,t,m,g}^{u,v}
as the following event:
there exist
-g\leq b_{1}<b_{2}<b_{3}<b_{4}<b_{5}<b_{6}\leq g
, with
b_{2}-b_{1},b_{3}-b_{2},b_{4}-b_{3},b_{5}-b_{4},b_{6}-b_{5}\geq\lambda
, such that
T_{u,v}=T_{u,\langle m,b_{1}\rangle}^{\bullet}+T_{\langle m,b_{1}\rangle,v}
, and

T_{u,\langle m,b_{i}\rangle}^{\bullet}+T_{\langle m,b_{i}\rangle,v}>T_{u,v}-t\lambda^{1/2},\quad\forall i\in\{2,3,4,5,6\}.

Lemma 8.5
.

For
h>0
and
0<\kappa<1/2
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that the following is true.
For any
\theta>0
,
0<t<1
,
\kappa<\alpha<1-\kappa
,
|\beta|<h
,
we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{M}_{\theta n^{2/3},t,\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor,hn^{2/3}}^{\mathbf{0},\langle n,\lfloor\beta n^{2/3}\rfloor\rangle}]<Ct^{5-0.01}
,
for
n
large enough depending on
h,\theta,t,\alpha,\beta
.

Lemma
8.4
will be proved in Section
8.2
, and Lemma
8.5
will be proved in Section
8.1
.

Assuming all the lemmas above, we now prove Proposition
8.1
.
We set up the events to be used in the proof of Proposition
8.1
, for which we first define the parameters.

Parameters:

From now on we fix
h
in the statement of Proposition
8.1
.
As indicated above, we will choose vertices
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},u_{4},u_{5}
in
\mathfrak{P}_{1}
, in a multi-scale way.
Thus we define the scales as follows. We take a small number
\delta>0
, and let
\delta_{i}=\delta^{100^{6-i}}
for
i=0,1,2,3,4,5
.
So we have
0<\delta_{0}<\delta_{1}<\delta_{2}<\delta_{3}<\delta_{4}<\delta_{5}<\delta
. We also take small
\kappa>0
and large
\hat{h}
, and we can assume that
\delta
is small enough depending on
\kappa
and
\hat{h}
, and
\hat{h}
is large enough depending on
h
. The values of the parameters
\delta,\kappa,\hat{h}
are to be determined, but we always ensure that
\delta^{-1},\kappa^{-1},\hat{h}
are integers.
Then there exists some integer
N
, such that if we denote
\mathscr{N}=\{Nk^{3}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
, for any
n\in\mathscr{N}
we have
\delta_{0}n,\delta_{0}n^{2/3},\hat{h}n^{2/3},\kappa n,\delta^{-1}
, and each
\delta_{i}^{-1}\delta_{i+1}
for
i\in\{0,1,2,3,4\}
are integers.
From now on we assume that
n\in\mathscr{N}
, and is large enough depending on all these parameters. Only inside the proof of Proposition
8.1
will we treat general large
n
.

Below we use
c,C>0
to denote small and large enough constants, which can only depend on
\hat{h}
and
\kappa
, and the values may change from line to line.

Events:
We take the two families of vertices as
\mathfrak{P}_{1}=\{\langle i\delta_{0}n,j\delta_{0}n^{2/3}\rangle:i,j\in\mathbb{Z},0\leq i\leq\delta_{0}^{-1}/3,|j|<4\hat{h}\delta_{0}^{-1}\}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{2}=\mathbf{n}-\mathfrak{P}_{1}
.
Note that here we take
\mathfrak{P}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{2}
to be in rectangles with width in the order of
\hat{h}n^{2/3}
rather than
hn^{2/3}
, because the geodesics (that we will study) can potentially have large transversal fluctuations.
Consider the following events.

•

Let
\mathcal{T}
be the union of
\mathcal{T}^{u,v}_{l,\delta^{-1}}
, for all
u\in\mathfrak{P}_{1},v\in\mathfrak{P}_{2}
, and
l\in\delta_{0}n\mathbb{Z}
,
0\leq l<d(v)-d(u)
.
By Lemma
8.3
we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{T}]<C\delta_{0}^{-5}e^{-c\delta^{-3}}
.

•

Let
\mathcal{T}_{*}=\mathcal{T}^{\langle 0,\hat{h}n^{2/3}\rangle,\langle n,\hat{h}n^{2/3}\rangle}_{n,\hat{h}}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\langle 0,-\hat{h}n^{2/3}\rangle,\langle n,-\hat{h}n^{2/3}\rangle}_{n,\hat{h}}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\langle 0,3\hat{h}n^{2/3}\rangle,\langle n,3\hat{h}n^{2/3}\rangle}_{n,\hat{h}}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\langle 0,-3\hat{h}n^{2/3}\rangle,\langle n,-3\hat{h}n^{2/3}\rangle}_{n,\hat{h}}
.
In other words,
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{c}
is just the event where for each
j\in\{-3,-1,1,3\}
the geodesic
\Gamma_{\langle 0,j\hat{h}n^{2/3}\rangle,\langle n,j\hat{h}n^{2/3}\rangle}
is contained in
\{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:0\leq d(u)\leq 2n,|ad(u)-2j\hat{h}n^{2/3}|\leq 2\hat{h}n^{2/3}\}
.
By Lemma
8.3
we have that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{T}_{*}]\to 0
as
\hat{h}\to\infty
, uniformly in
n
.

•

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the event where

|T_{\langle i\delta_{1}n,a\rangle,\langle j\delta_{1}n,b\rangle}^{\bullet}-T_{\langle i\delta_{1}n,a^{\prime}\rangle,\langle j\delta_{1}n,b^{\prime}\rangle}^{\bullet}|>\delta_{0}^{1/2-0.02}n^{1/3}

for some integers
0\leq i<j\leq\delta_{1}^{-1}
, and
|a|,|a^{\prime}|,|b|,|b^{\prime}|\leq 4\hat{h}n^{2/3}
with
|a-a^{\prime}|,|b-b^{\prime}|\leq\delta_{0}n^{2/3}
.
By applying Lemma
8.2
to this event with each fixed
i,j
and taking a union bound,
we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{F}]<C\delta_{1}^{-2-2/3}e^{-c\delta_{0}^{-0.01}}
.

•

Let
\mathcal{D}
be the union of
\mathcal{D}^{u,v}_{\delta^{-7},l,m}
for all
u\in\mathfrak{P}_{1}
,
v\in\mathfrak{P}_{2}
,
l\in\{\delta_{i}n:i=1,2,3,4,5\}
,
m\in\delta_{0}n\mathbb{Z}
, such that
d(u)\leq 2m<2m+2\delta^{-7}l\leq d(v)
.
Here we take
c_{0}
to be small enough as required by Lemma
8.4
.
Then by applying Lemma
8.4
to each
\mathcal{D}^{u,v}_{\delta^{-7},l,m}
and taking a union bound, we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{D}]<C\delta_{0}^{-5}e^{-c\delta^{-7}}
.

•

Let
\mathcal{H}
denote the event where there exists some
m\in\delta_{0}n\mathbb{Z}
,
0\leq m\leq n
, and
l\in\{\delta_{i}n:i=1,2,3,4,5\}
,
|a|,|b|<4\hat{h}n^{2/3}
,
|a-b|<\delta^{-6}l^{2/3}
, such that

T_{\langle m,a\rangle,\langle m+\delta^{-7}l,b\rangle}<4\delta^{-7}l-c_{0}\delta^{-6}l^{1/3},

where
c_{0}
is the same as in the event
\mathcal{D}
.
By applying Proposition
5.3
via splitting the lines
\mathbb{L}_{m}
and
\mathbb{L}_{m+\delta^{-7}l}
into segments of length
\delta_{0}n^{2/3}
, we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{H}]<C\delta_{0}^{-3}e^{-c\delta^{-11/2}}
.

•

Let
\mathcal{M}
be the union of
\mathcal{M}^{u,v}_{c_{0}(\delta_{1}n)^{2/3},\delta_{0}^{1/2-0.03}\delta_{1}^{-1/3},\alpha n,4\hat{h}n^{2/3}}
, for all
u\in\mathfrak{P}_{1}\cap\mathbb{L}_{0}
,
v\in\mathfrak{P}_{2}\cap\mathbb{L}_{n}
, and
\alpha\in\delta_{1}\mathbb{Z}
with
\kappa<\alpha<1-\kappa
, and
c_{0}
be the same as in the event
\mathcal{D}
.
By Lemma
8.5
, we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{M}]<C\delta_{0}^{-2}\delta_{1}^{-1}(\delta_{0}^{1/2-0.03}\delta_{1}^{-1/3})^{5-0.01}<C\delta_{0}^{0.3}\delta_{1}^{-8/3}
.

We denote
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{T}_{*}^{c}\cap\mathcal{F}^{c}\cap\mathcal{D}^{c}\cap\mathcal{H}^{c}\cap\mathcal{M}^{c}
.
These events are designed so that
\mathcal{E}
happens with high probability, and under
\mathcal{E}
we have covering of geodesics.

Lemma 8.6
.

Under
\mathcal{E}
the following holds:
for any
|a|,|b|<\hat{h}n^{2/3}
, there exist
u\in\mathfrak{P}_{1}
and
v\in\mathfrak{P}_{2}
, with
d(u)<4\kappa n
and
d(v)>(1-4\kappa)n
, such that
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
is the same as
\Gamma_{u,v}
between
\mathbb{L}_{2\kappa n}
and
\mathbb{L}_{(1-2\kappa)n}
.

Proof.

Assume that
\mathcal{E}
holds, and fix
a,b
such that
|a|,|b|<\hat{h}n^{2/3}
.
By ordering of geodesics (Lemma
5.1
),
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
is between
\Gamma_{\langle 0,-\hat{h}n^{2/3}\rangle,\langle n,-\hat{h}n^{2/3}\rangle}
and
\Gamma_{\langle 0,\hat{h}n^{2/3}\rangle,\langle n,\hat{h}n^{2/3}\rangle}
.
Then by
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{c}
, we have

\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}\subset\{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:0\leq d(u)\leq 2n,|ad(u)|\leq 4\hat{h}n^{2/3}\}.

(8.1)

Let
b^{+}
be the smallest number with
b^{+}\in\delta_{0}n^{2/3}\mathbb{Z}
and
b^{+}\geq b
.
As indicated above, we now show that we can find
u^{*}\in\mathfrak{P}_{1}
with
d(u^{*})<4\kappa n
, such that there exists
u\in\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
with
d(u)=d(u^{*})
and
ad(u)\leq ad(u^{*})\leq ad(u)+2\delta_{0}n^{2/3}
,
and

\Gamma_{u^{*},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}
intersects
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
before
\mathbb{L}_{2\kappa n}
.

For this we argue by contradiction, and assume that no such
u^{*}
exists.
We will sequentially find the vertices
u_{5},u_{4},u_{3},u_{2},u_{1}
(as illustrated in Figure
17
) and then use them to find some multiple peaks, thus get a contradiction with
\mathcal{M}^{c}
.
The idea is to take each
u_{i}
as the vertex in
\mathfrak{P}_{1}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{i}n}
that is to the right of and closest to
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
.
Here
\alpha_{i}
are numbers to be chosen sequentially: given
u_{i+1}
, we find
\alpha_{i}
such that the intersections of
\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{i}}
with
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
and
\Gamma_{u_{i+1},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}
are
c_{0}(\delta_{i+1}n)^{2/3}
apart, using
\mathcal{D}^{c}
.
Finally we consider the intersections of each
\Gamma_{u_{i},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}
with
\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{0}n}
: we can ensure that they are still
c_{0}(\delta_{1}n)^{2/3}
apart from each other, using transversal fluctuation bounds (from event
\mathcal{T}^{c}
) and the fact that
\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{i}
is chosen to be in the order of
\delta_{i}
.

Sequential construction.
Let’s start by choosing
u_{5}
.
We take
\alpha_{5}
as the smallest number such that
\alpha_{5}\in\delta_{5}\mathbb{Z}
and
\alpha_{5}>\kappa
, and take
u_{5}\in\mathfrak{P}_{1}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{5}n}
being the first one on or to the right of
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
.
In other words, we have

0\leq ad(u_{5})-ad(\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{5}n})<2\delta_{0}n^{2/3}
.
Then by (
8.1
), we have that
|ad(u_{5})|\leq 4\hat{h}n^{2/3}
.
Consider the path
\Gamma_{u_{5},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}
.
Again by
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{c}
and ordering of geodesics (Lemma
5.1
), it is between
\Gamma_{\langle 0,-3\hat{h}n^{2/3}\rangle,\langle n,-3\hat{h}n^{2/3}\rangle}
and
\Gamma_{\langle 0,3\hat{h}n^{2/3}\rangle,\langle n,3\hat{h}n^{2/3}\rangle}
and

\Gamma_{u_{5},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}\subset\{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:0\leq d(u)\leq 2n,|ad(u)|\leq 8\hat{h}n^{2/3}\}.

(8.2)

For each
j\in\llbracket 0,\delta^{-7}\rrbracket
, we have

ad(\mathbb{L}_{(\alpha_{5}+j\delta_{5})n}\cap\Gamma_{u_{5},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle})-ad(\mathbb{L}_{(\alpha_{5}+j\delta_{5})n}\cap\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle})\geq 0,

by ordering of geodesics (Lemma
5.1
).
We claim that there must exist
j_{5}\in\llbracket 0,\delta^{-7}\rrbracket
, such that the left-hand side for
j=j_{5}
is at least
2c_{0}(\delta_{5}n)^{2/3}
.
Indeed, otherwise we can show that the event
\mathcal{D}^{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}_{\delta^{-7},\delta_{5}n,\alpha_{5}n}
holds with the path being
\Gamma_{u_{5},w_{5}}
, where
w_{5}=\Gamma_{u_{5},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}\cap\mathbb{L}_{(\alpha_{5}+\delta^{-7}\delta_{5})n}
(see Figure
17
).
For this we just verify several things:

•

By the assumption above (that no such
u^{*}
exists),
\Gamma_{u_{5},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}
is disjoint from
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
before
\mathbb{L}_{2\kappa n}
, thus
\Gamma_{u_{5},w_{5}}
is disjoint from
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
since by taking
\delta
small enough depending on
\kappa
we have
\alpha_{5}+\delta^{-7}\delta_{5}<2\kappa
.

•

We have
|ad(w_{5})|\leq 8\hat{h}n^{2/3}
by (
8.2
).
By
\mathcal{T}^{c}
we have that

|ad(u_{5})-ad(w_{5})|<2\delta^{-1}(\delta^{-7}\delta_{5}n)^{2/3}=2\delta^{-17/3}(\delta_{5}n)^{2/3}.

Then we have
T_{u_{5},w_{5}}\geq 4\delta^{-7}\delta_{5}n-c_{0}\delta^{-6}(\delta_{5}n)^{1/3}
by
\mathcal{H}^{c}
.

Thus the event
\mathcal{D}^{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}_{\delta^{-7},\delta_{5}n,\alpha_{5}n}
holds, contradicting with
\mathcal{D}^{c}
. So such
j_{5}
must exist.

We next let
\alpha_{4}=\alpha_{5}+j_{5}\delta_{5}
,
and take
u_{4}\in\mathfrak{P}_{1}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{4}n}
being the first one on or to the right of
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
.
Using the same arguments we find
0\leq j_{4}\leq\delta^{-7}
, such that

|ad(\mathbb{L}_{(\alpha_{4}+j_{4}\delta_{4})n}\cap\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle})-ad(\mathbb{L}_{(\alpha_{4}+j_{4}\delta_{4})n}\cap\Gamma_{u_{4},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle})|\geq 2c_{0}(\delta_{4}n)^{2/3}.

Then we let
\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{4}+j_{4}\delta_{4}
.
Similarly we find
j_{3},j_{2},j_{1}\in\llbracket 0,\delta^{-7}\rrbracket
, and
\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{3}+j_{3}\delta_{3}
,
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}+j_{2}\delta_{2}
,
\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{1}+j_{1}\delta_{1}
, and vertices
u_{3}\in\mathfrak{P}_{1}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{3}n}
,
u_{2}\in\mathfrak{P}_{1}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{2}n}
,
u_{1}\in\mathfrak{P}_{1}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{1}n}
, such that for each
i=1,2,3
we have

0\leq ad(u_{i})-ad(\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{i}n}\cap\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle})<2\delta_{0}n^{2/3},

(8.3)

and

ad(\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{i-1}n}\cap\Gamma_{u_{i},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle})-ad(\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{i-1}n}\cap\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle})\geq 2c_{0}(\delta_{i}n)^{2/3}.

(8.4)

Note that
\mathbb{L}_{(\alpha_{i}+j_{i}\delta_{i})n}=\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{i-1}n}
for each
i=1,2,3,4,5
, and that (
8.3
) and (
8.4
) also hold for
i=4,5
as stated above.
See Figure
17
for (some of) these constructed objects.

u_{5}
u_{4}
u_{3}
w_{5}
\mathbb{L}_{\kappa n}
\mathbb{L}_{0}
\mathbb{L}_{(\alpha_{5}+\delta^{-7}\delta_{5})n}
\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{0}n}
\mathbb{L}_{(\alpha_{5}+j_{5}\delta_{5})n}=\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{4}n}
\langle\alpha_{0}n,b_{5}\rangle
\langle\alpha_{0}n,b_{4}\rangle
\langle\alpha_{0}n,b_{0}\rangle
\langle 0,a\rangle
\langle 0,a^{-}\rangle
\langle n,b\rangle
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
\Gamma_{u_{3},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}
\Gamma_{u_{4},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}
\Gamma_{u_{5},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}

Figure 17
:
An illustration of the geodesics
\Gamma_{u_{i},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}
for
i=5,4,3
. Their intersections with
\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{0}n}
are separated by
c_{0}(\delta_{1}n)^{2/3}
.

Multiple peaks event.

We denote the intersections of
\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{0}n}
with
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
and
\Gamma_{u_{i},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}
to be
\langle\alpha_{0}n,b_{0}\rangle
and
\langle\alpha_{0}n,b_{i}\rangle
, for
i=1,2,3,4,5
.
We next lower bound the differences between these
b_{i}
.

From (
8.4
) we have that
b_{1}-b_{0}\geq c_{0}(\delta_{1}n)^{2/3}
.
We next show that
b_{i}-b_{i-1}\geq c_{0}(\delta_{1}n)^{2/3}
, for each
i=2,3,4,5
.
By
\mathcal{T}^{c}
and considering
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
above
\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{i}n}
and
\Gamma_{u_{i},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}
, and using (
8.3
), we have

b_{i}-b_{0}<\delta_{0}n^{2/3}+2\delta^{-1}(\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{i})^{2/3}n^{2/3}<\delta_{0}n^{2/3}+2\delta^{-1}(2\delta^{-7}\delta_{i}n)^{2/3},

for each
i=1,2,3,4,5
, where the last inequality is by
\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{i}\leq\delta^{-7}\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{i}\delta_{i^{\prime}}<2\delta^{-7}\delta_{i}
.
Similarly, by
\mathcal{T}^{c}
and considering
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
and
\Gamma_{u_{i},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}
above
\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{i-1}n}
, and using (
8.4
), we have

b_{i}-b_{0}\geq c_{0}(\delta_{i}n)^{2/3}-2\delta_{0}n^{2/3}-2\delta^{-1}(\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{i-1})^{2/3}n^{2/3}>c_{0}(\delta_{i}n)^{2/3}-2\delta_{0}n^{2/3}-2\delta^{-1}(2\delta^{-7}\delta_{i-1}n)^{2/3},

for each
i=2,3,4,5
.
Thus we get that

b_{i}-b_{i-1}>c_{0}(\delta_{i}n)^{2/3}-3\delta_{0}n^{2/3}-4\delta^{-1}(2\delta^{-7}\delta_{i-1}n)^{2/3}>c_{0}(\delta_{1}n)^{2/3},

for each
i=2,3,4,5
.

Besides, since
|b_{0}|\leq 2\hat{h}n^{2/3}
(by (
8.1
)), we have that
-2\hat{h}n^{2/3}\leq b_{0}<b_{5}<(2\hat{h}+1)n^{2/3}
.

To obtain the multiple peaks event at these
b_{i}
, the remaining task is to bound the passage times through each
\langle\alpha_{0}n,b_{i}\rangle
, from
\langle 0,a^{-}\rangle
to
\langle n,b^{-}\rangle
, where
a^{-},b^{-}
are the largest numbers satisfying
a^{-},b^{-}\in\delta_{0}n^{2/3}\mathbb{Z}
and
a^{-}\leq a
,
b^{-}\leq b
.
Recall that we denote
T_{u,v}^{\bullet}=T_{u,v}-\xi(v)
for any vertices
u\leq v
.
For each
i=1,2,3,4,5
, denote
u_{i}^{\prime}=\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{i}n}
, and we have
|ad(u_{i})|,|ad(u_{i}^{\prime})|\leq 4\hat{h}n^{2/3}
by (
8.1
) and (
8.3
). We then have

\begin{split}&T_{\langle 0,a^{-}\rangle,\langle\alpha_{0}n,b_{i}\rangle}^{\bullet}+T_{\langle\alpha_{0}n,b_{i}\rangle,\langle n,b^{-}\rangle}\\
\geq&T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle\alpha_{0}n,b_{i}\rangle}^{\bullet}+T_{\langle\alpha_{0}n,b_{i}\rangle,\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}-2\delta_{0}^{1/2-0.02}n^{1/3}\\
\geq&T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,u_{i}^{\prime}}^{\bullet}+T_{u_{i}^{\prime},\langle\alpha_{0}n,b_{i}\rangle}^{\bullet}+T_{\langle\alpha_{0}n,b_{i}\rangle,\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}-2\delta_{0}^{1/2-0.02}n^{1/3}\\
\geq&T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,u_{i}^{\prime}}^{\bullet}+T_{u_{i},\langle\alpha_{0}n,b_{i}\rangle}^{\bullet}+T_{\langle\alpha_{0}n,b_{i}\rangle,\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}-3\delta_{0}^{1/2-0.02}n^{1/3}\\
=&T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,u_{i}^{\prime}}^{\bullet}+T_{u_{i},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}-3\delta_{0}^{1/2-0.02}n^{1/3}\\
\geq&T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,u_{i}^{\prime}}^{\bullet}+T_{u_{i}^{\prime},\langle n,b\rangle}-4\delta_{0}^{1/2-0.02}n^{1/3}\\
=&T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}-4\delta_{0}^{1/2-0.02}n^{1/3}\\
\geq&T_{\langle 0,a^{-}\rangle,\langle n,b^{-}\rangle}-5\delta_{0}^{1/2-0.02}n^{1/3},\end{split}

where the second inequality is by
T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle\alpha_{0}n,b_{i}\rangle}^{\bullet}\geq T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,u_{i}^{\prime}}^{\bullet}+T_{u_{i}^{\prime},\langle\alpha_{0}n,b_{i}\rangle}^{\bullet}
which follows from the definition of passage times, and every other inequality is due to
\mathcal{F}^{c}
.
Note that if
\langle\alpha_{0}n,b_{0}^{-}\rangle
is the intersection of
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a^{-}\rangle,\langle n,b^{-}\rangle}
with
\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{0}n}
, then
-2\hat{h}n^{2/3}\leq b_{0}^{-}\leq b_{0}
by
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{c}
and ordering of geodesics (Lemma
5.1
).
Then we have that
\mathcal{M}^{\langle 0,a^{-}\rangle,\langle n,b^{-}\rangle}_{c_{0}(\delta_{1}n)^{2/3},\delta_{0}^{1/2-0.03}\delta_{1}^{-1/3},\alpha_{0}n,4\hat{h}n^{2/3}}
holds with
b_{0}^{-}<b_{1}<b_{2}<b_{3}<b_{4}<b_{5}
.
Also note that
\alpha_{0}\geq\alpha_{5}>\kappa
, and

\alpha_{0}\leq\alpha_{5}+\sum_{i=1}^{5}\delta^{-7}\delta_{i}<\alpha_{5}+2\delta^{-7}\delta_{5}\leq\kappa+\delta_{5}+2\delta^{-7}\delta_{5}<1-\kappa.

Thus we get a contradiction with
\mathcal{M}^{c}
.
Now we conclude that there exists
u^{*}\in\mathfrak{P}_{1}
with
d(u^{*})<4\kappa n
, such that there is
u\in\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
with
d(u)=d(u^{*})
and
ad(u)\leq ad(u^{*})\leq ad(u)+2\delta_{0}n^{2/3}
, and
\Gamma_{u^{*},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}
intersects
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
before
\mathbb{L}_{2\kappa n}
.

Final steps.

Using the same arguments, we can find
v^{*}\in\mathfrak{P}_{2}
with
d(v^{*})>(2-4\kappa)n
, such that there is
v\in\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
with
d(v)=d(v^{*})
and
ad(v)\leq ad(v^{*})\leq ad(v)+2\delta_{0}n^{2/3}
, and
\Gamma_{u^{*},v^{*}}
intersects
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
after
\mathbb{L}_{(1-2\kappa)n}
.
We now consider the geodesics
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
,
\Gamma_{u^{*},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}
, and
\Gamma_{u^{*},v^{*}}
, between
\mathbb{L}_{2\kappa n}
and
\mathbb{L}_{(1-2\kappa)n}
.
By ordering of geodesics (Lemma
5.1
), we have that either
\Gamma_{u^{*},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}
is sandwiched between
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
and
\Gamma_{u^{*},v^{*}}
, or
\Gamma_{u^{*},v^{*}}
is sandwiched between
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
and
\Gamma_{u^{*},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}
.
In the former case we have that
\Gamma_{u^{*},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}
intersects
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
before
\mathbb{L}_{2\kappa n}
and after
\mathbb{L}_{(1-2\kappa)n}
, so
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
is the same as
\Gamma_{u^{*},\langle n,b^{+}\rangle}
between
\mathbb{L}_{2\kappa n}
and
\mathbb{L}_{(1-2\kappa)n}
;
in the later case we have that
\Gamma_{u^{*},v^{*}}
intersects
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
before
\mathbb{L}_{2\kappa n}
and after
\mathbb{L}_{(1-2\kappa)n}
, so
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
is the same as
\Gamma_{u^{*},v^{*}}
between
\mathbb{L}_{2\kappa n}
and
\mathbb{L}_{(1-2\kappa)n}
. Thus the conclusion follows.
∎

We can now finish the proof of Proposition
8.1
using Lemma
8.6
.

Proof of Proposition
8.1
.

As stated above we write the proof for
\rho=1/2
for simplicity of notations.

We now consider general
n
, i.e. not necessarily in
\mathscr{N}
.
We let
n^{\prime}
be the largest number such that
n^{\prime}\leq n
and
n^{\prime}\in\mathscr{N}
.
Then we have that
n^{\prime}\to\infty
and
n^{\prime}/n\to 1
as
n\to\infty
.
We define
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
as
\mathcal{E}
for
n^{\prime}
instead of
n
, and
\mathfrak{P}_{1}^{\prime}
,
\mathfrak{P}_{2}^{\prime}
as
\mathfrak{P}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{P}_{2}
for
n^{\prime}
instead of
n
.

By Theorem
7.3
, as
n\to\infty
we have

\max_{u\in\mathfrak{P}_{1}^{\prime},v\in\mathfrak{P}_{2}^{\prime}}|\mu_{u,v}(f)-\nu(f)|\to 0,

in probability. Thus by Lemma
8.6
, and that
f
is a bounded function on
\mathbb{R}^{\llbracket-s,s\rrbracket^{2}}\times\{0,1\}^{\llbracket-s,s\rrbracket^{2}}
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\max_{a,b\in\mathbb{Z},|a|,|b|<\hat{h}{n^{\prime}}^{2/3}}|\mu_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n^{\prime},b\rangle}(f)-\nu(f)|>10\kappa\|f\|_{\infty}\right]\to 0.

Denote
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}^{\langle 0,\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle,\langle n,\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle}_{n-n^{\prime},\hat{h}{n^{\prime}}^{2/3}(n-n^{\prime})^{-2/3}/2}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\langle 0,-\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle,\langle n,-\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle}_{n-n^{\prime},\hat{h}{n^{\prime}}^{2/3}(n-n^{\prime})^{-2/3}/2}
.
By ordering of geodesics (Lemma
5.1
), we have that
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
for
|a|,|b|<hn^{2/3}
is sandwiched between
\Gamma_{\langle 0,-\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle,\langle n,-\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle}
and
\Gamma_{\langle 0,\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle,\langle n,\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle}
; so assuming the complement of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, we must have that for any
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
with
|a|,|b|<hn^{2/3}
, it intersects
\mathbb{L}_{n^{\prime}}
at some vertex
\langle n^{\prime},b^{\prime}\rangle
with

|b^{\prime}|\leq\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil+\hat{h}{n^{\prime}}^{2/3}/2<\hat{h}{n^{\prime}}^{2/3},

where the second inequality is by taking
\hat{h}
much larger than
h
.
Thus we have

\mathbb{P}\Big[\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}^{c},\max_{a,b\in\mathbb{Z},|a|,|b|<hn^{2/3}/2}|\mu_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}(f)-\nu(f)|>(10\kappa+2(n-n^{\prime})/n)\|f\|_{\infty}\Big]\to 0.

Then since
(n-n^{\prime})/n\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Big[\max_{a,b\in\mathbb{Z},|a|,|b|<hn^{2/3}/2}|\mu_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}(f)-\nu(f)|>11\kappa\|f\|_{\infty}\Big]\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]+\mathbb{P}[{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}^{c}].

(8.5)

By Lemma
8.3
, we have
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]=0
.
Also, by the discussion of the events
\mathcal{T}
,
\mathcal{T}_{*}
,
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{D}
,
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{M}
before Lemma
8.6
, we have that
\lim_{\hat{h}\to\infty}\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}[{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}^{c}]=0
.
Thus we conclude that the left-hand side of (
8.5
) equals
0
. Then since
\kappa
can be arbitraily taken, the conclusion follows.
∎

For the next two subsections we prove Lemmas
8.2
,
8.4
, and
8.5
.

8.1
Continuity of passage times and multiple peaks

In this subsection we prove Lemma
8.2
and
8.5
.
For both of them we use the convergence of the point-to-line profile to the Airy
2
process, which is a stationary ergodic process minus a parabola.
Such convergence in the sense of finite dimensional distributions is from
[
BF08
,
BP08
]
.
Using the so-called slow decorrelation phenomenon, and proving equicontinuity of the point-to-line profile,
it also follows that the weak convergence holds in the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets
[
BGZ21
,
FO18
]
.
More precisely, let
\mathcal{A}_{2}
denote the stationary Airy
2
process on
\mathbb{R}
, and let us define the stochastic process
\mathcal{L}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\mathcal{L}(x):=\mathcal{A}_{2}(x)-x^{2}.

We quote the following result.

Theorem 8.7

(
[
BGZ21
, Theorem 3.8]
)
.

Consider the function

\mathcal{L}_{n}:x\mapsto 2^{-4/3}n^{-1/3}\left(T_{\mathbf{0},\langle n,x(2n)^{2/3}\rangle}-4n\right),

where we linearly interpolate between points in
(2n)^{-2/3}\mathbb{Z}
.
As
n\to\infty
, we have
\mathcal{L}_{n}\to\mathcal{L}
weakly in the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets.

We shall also use the following (quantitative) comparison between the Airy
2
process, and a Brownian motion.

For
K\in\mathbb{R},d>0
, let
\mathcal{B}^{[K,K+d]}
denote the law of a Brownian motion with diffusivity
2
on
[K,K+d]
, taking value
0
at
K
. Let
\mathcal{L}^{[K,K+d]}
denote the random function on
[K,K+d]
defined by

\mathcal{L}^{[K,K+d]}(x):=\mathcal{L}(x)-\mathcal{L}(K),\penalty\ \forall x\in[K,K+d].

Let
C\big([K,K+d],\mathbb{R}\big)
denote the space of all real-valued continuous functions defined on
[K,K+d]
which vanish at
K
, with the topology of uniform convergence. The following result can be obtained from
[
CHH
]
.

Theorem 8.8

(
[
CHH
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

There exists an universal constant
G>0
such that the following holds.
For any fixed
M>0,
there exists
a_{0}=a_{0}(M)
such that for all intervals
[K,K+d]\subset[-M,M]
and for all measurable
A\subset C\big([K,K+d],\mathbb{R}\big)
with
0<\mathcal{B}^{[K,K+d]}(A)=a\leq a_{0},

\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{L}^{[K,K+d]}\in A\right]\leq ae^{G{M}(\log a^{-1})^{5/6}}.

Now we prove Lemma
8.2
.
We start with the following estimate on deviations when moving one endpoint.

Lemma 8.9
.

There are constants
c,C>0
such that the following holds.
For any
h\in\mathbb{R}
,
0\theta<1
, and
t>1
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{hn^{2/3}<b,b^{\prime}<(h+1)n^{2/3},|b-b^{\prime}|<\theta n^{2/3}}|T_{\mathbf{0},\langle n,b\rangle}-T_{\mathbf{0},\langle n,b^{\prime}\rangle}|>t\theta^{1/2-0.01}n^{1/3}+C(|h|+1)\theta n^{1/3}\right]<Ce^{-ct}

(8.6)

for
n
large enough (depending on
h,\theta,t
).

Proof.

For any continuous function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, we let

\mathscr{M}(f):=\max_{2^{-2/3}h\leq x,x^{\prime}\leq 2^{-2/3}(h+1),|x-x^{\prime}|\leq 2^{-2/3}\theta}|f(x)-f(x^{\prime})|.

It is straightforward to check that
\mathscr{M}
is a continuous functional on the space of all continuous real-valued functions on
\mathbb{R}
, with the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets.

By Theorem
8.8
,
\mathscr{M}(\mathcal{L})
has continuous distribution since this is the case when
\mathcal{L}
is replaced by a Brownian motion.
Thus by Theorem
8.7
, as
n\to\infty
we have

\mathbb{P}[\mathscr{M}(\mathcal{L}_{n})>x]\to\mathbb{P}[\mathscr{M}(\mathcal{L})>x]
for any
x>0
.
We note that the left-hand side of (
8.6
) is bounded by

\mathbb{P}[\mathscr{M}(\mathcal{L}_{n})>2^{-4/3}t\theta^{1/2-0.01}+2^{-4/3}C(|h|+1)\theta].

Thus as
n\to\infty
, the
\limsup
of the left-hand side of (
8.6
) is bounded by

\mathbb{P}[\mathscr{M}(\mathcal{L})>2^{-4/3}t\theta^{1/2-0.01}+2^{-4/3}C(|h|+1)\theta].

We next show that this is bounded by
Ce^{-ct}
.
When
C>2
we have
|x^{2}-x^{\prime 2}|<2^{-4/3}C(|h|+1)\theta
for all
x,x^{\prime}
with
2^{-2/3}h\leq x,x^{\prime}\leq 2^{-2/3}(h+1)
and
|x-x^{\prime}|\leq 2^{-2/3}\theta
.
Then by stationarity of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
, we can bound this probability by

\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{0\leq x,x^{\prime}\leq 2^{-2/3},|x-x^{\prime}|\leq 2^{-2/3}\theta}|\mathcal{L}(x)-\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime})|>2^{-4/3}t\theta^{1/2-0.01}\right].

Note that the event now only relies on
\mathcal{L}^{[0,2^{-2/3}]}
.
Using modulus of continuity for Brownian motions and Theorem
8.8
, we can bound this by
Ce^{-ct}
as desired.
∎

We can now prove Lemma
8.2
by using Lemma
8.9
repeatedly.

Proof of Lemma
8.2
.

First, note that we have the following inequality for passage times:

T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}-T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b^{\prime}\rangle}\geq T_{\langle 0,a^{\prime}\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}-T_{\langle 0,a^{\prime}\rangle,\langle n,b^{\prime}\rangle}

for any
a\leq a^{\prime},b\leq b^{\prime}
.
Indeed, if we take the geodesics
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b^{\prime}\rangle}
and
\Gamma_{\langle 0,a^{\prime}\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}
, then they must intersect. By switching the paths after their first intersection, we get two up-right paths, from
\langle 0,a\rangle
to
\langle n,b\rangle
and from
\langle 0,a^{\prime}\rangle
to
\langle n,b^{\prime}\rangle
, and the sum of their passage times equals
T_{\langle 0,a^{\prime}\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}+T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b^{\prime}\rangle}
.
Thus we get the above inequality from the definition of last-passage times.

Using this inequality, for any
|a|,|a^{\prime}|,|b|,|b^{\prime}|<hn^{2/3}
we have

|T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}-T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b^{\prime}\rangle}|\leq|T_{\langle 0,-\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}-T_{\langle 0,-\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle,\langle n,b^{\prime}\rangle}|\vee|T_{\langle 0,\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}-T_{\langle 0,\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle,\langle n,b^{\prime}\rangle}|,

and

|T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b^{\prime}\rangle}-T_{\langle 0,a^{\prime}\rangle,\langle n,b^{\prime}\rangle}|\leq|T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,-\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle}-T_{\langle 0,a^{\prime}\rangle,\langle n,-\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle}|\vee|T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle}-T_{\langle 0,a^{\prime}\rangle,\langle n,\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle}|.

By adding up these two inequalities and using the triangle inequality, we have

\begin{split}|T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}-T_{\langle 0,a^{\prime}\rangle,\langle n,b^{\prime}\rangle}|\leq&|T_{\langle 0,-\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}-T_{\langle 0,-\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle,\langle n,b^{\prime}\rangle}|\vee|T_{\langle 0,\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}-T_{\langle 0,\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle,\langle n,b^{\prime}\rangle}|\\
+&|T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,-\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle}-T_{\langle 0,a^{\prime}\rangle,\langle n,-\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle}|\vee|T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle n,\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle}-T_{\langle 0,a^{\prime}\rangle,\langle n,\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle}|\end{split}

By symmetry, now it suffices to bound

\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}|b|,|b^{\prime}|<hn^{2/3}\\
|b-b^{\prime}|<\theta n^{2/3}\end{subarray}}|T_{\langle 0,-\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle,\langle n,b\rangle}-T_{\langle 0,-\lceil hn^{2/3}\rceil\rangle,\langle n,b^{\prime}\rangle}|>\frac{1}{2}(t\theta^{1/2-0.01}n^{1/3}+Ch\theta n^{1/3})\right].

For this we split
\{\langle 0,b\rangle:|b|<hn^{2/3}\}
into overlapping segments of length
n^{2/3}
, and apply Lemma
8.9
to each of them, and get the desired bound.
∎

We next prove Lemma
8.5
.
Again, using Theorem
8.7
we reduce the point-to-line profiles to Airy
2
processes, and then by applying Theorem
8.8
we can just prove the result for Brownian motions.

Proof of Lemma
8.5
.

Denote
\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\beta}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
as the process given by

\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\beta}(x):=\alpha^{1/3}\mathcal{L}(\alpha^{-2/3}x)+(1-\alpha)^{1/3}\mathcal{L}^{\prime}((1-\alpha)^{-2/3}(x-2^{-2/3}\beta)),

where
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is an independent copy of
\mathcal{L}
.
Denote

\mathcal{L}_{n,\alpha,\beta}(x):=2^{-4/3}n^{-1/3}\left(T_{\mathbf{0},\langle\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor,x(2n)^{2/3}\rangle}^{\bullet}+T_{\langle\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor,x(2n)^{2/3}\rangle,\langle n,\lfloor\beta n^{2/3}\rfloor\rangle}-4n\right),

where we linearly interpolate between points in
(2n)^{-2/3}\mathbb{Z}
.
Using Theorem
8.7
, we can deduce that
\mathcal{L}_{n,\alpha,\beta}\to\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\beta}
as
n\to\infty
, weakly in the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets.

We let
\Omega
be the set of all continuous function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, such that there exist
-2^{1/3}h\leq x_{1}<x_{2}<x_{3}<x_{4}<x_{5}<x_{6}\leq 2^{1/3}h
, with
x_{2}-x_{1},x_{3}-x_{2},x_{4}-x_{3},x_{5}-x_{4},x_{6}-x_{5}\geq 2^{-2/3}\theta
, and
x_{1}=\argmax_{[-2^{4/3}h,2^{4/3}h]}f
, and

f(x_{1})\leq f(x_{i})+2^{-4/3}t\theta^{1/2},\quad\forall i=2,3,4,5,6.

It is straightforward to check that
\Omega
is a closed set, in the space of all continuous function with the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets.
It is also straightforward to check that
\mathcal{M}_{\theta n^{2/3},t,\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor,2hn^{2/3}}^{\mathbf{0},\langle n,\lfloor\beta n^{2/3}\rfloor\rangle}
implies
\mathcal{L}_{n,\alpha,\beta}\in\Omega
.
So by Theorem
8.7
we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{M}_{\theta n^{2/3},t,\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor,2hn^{2/3}}^{\mathbf{0},\langle n,\lfloor\beta n^{2/3}\rfloor\rangle}]\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{L}_{n,\alpha,\beta}\in\Omega]\leq\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\beta}\in\Omega].

We just need to bound the right-hand side.
By Theorem
8.8
, we can consider the probability of a (two-sided) Brownian motion (with diffusivity
4
) belonging to
\Omega
.
By Lemma
8.10
below this probability is bounded by
Ct^{5}
for
C>0
being a universal constant, so the conclusion follows.
∎

We finally bound the event on Brownian motions.

Lemma 8.10
.

There exists a universal constant
C>0
, such that for any
t,\theta>0
, the following event holds with probability at most
Ct^{5}
.
For
W:[-2,2]\to\mathbb{R}
being a two-sided Brownian motion, there are
-1<x_{1}<x_{2}<x_{3}<x_{4}<x_{5}<x_{6}<1
, with
x_{2}-x_{1},x_{3}-x_{2},x_{4}-x_{3},x_{5}-x_{4},x_{6}-x_{5}>\theta
, such that
x_{1}=\argmax_{[-2,2]}W
, and

W(x_{1})<W(x_{i})+t\theta^{1/2},\quad\forall i=2,3,4,5,6.

Proof.

Fix
T_{1}\in[-1,1]
, and let
\mathcal{E}
be the event where
W(T_{1})=\max_{[-2,2]}W
.
For
i=2,3,4,5,6
, let
T_{i}=\min\{x\geq T_{i-1}+\theta:W(x)\geq W(x_{1})-t\theta^{1/2}\}
.
It suffices to show that
\mathbb{P}[T_{6}\leq 1\mid\mathcal{E}]<Ct^{5}
for some universal constant
C>0
.
For
i=2,3,4,5,6
, conditioned on
\mathcal{E}
and the event
T_{i-1}\leq 1
, and given the values of
T_{i-1}
and
W(T_{i-1})-W(T_{1})
, the process
x\mapsto W(T_{i-1}+x)-W(T_{1})
on
[0,2-T_{i-1}]
has the same law of
W^{\prime}
, which is a Brownian motion on
[0,2-T_{i-1}]
starting from
W^{\prime}(0)=W(T_{i-1})-W(T_{1})
and conditioned to stay below zero (for
i=2
this degenerates to a Brownian meander). Using the reflection principle we have that
\mathbb{P}[\max_{[\theta,2-T_{i-1}]}W^{\prime}\geq-t\theta^{1/2}]<C^{\prime}t
for some universal constant
C^{\prime}>0
. So we have that
\mathbb{P}[T_{i}\leq 1\mid\mathcal{E},T_{i-1}\leq 1]<C^{\prime}t
. Thus
\mathbb{P}[T_{6}\leq 1\mid\mathcal{E}]<(C^{\prime}t)^{5}
, which implies the conclusion.
∎

8.2
Disjoint paths

In this subsection we prove Lemma
8.4
.
The idea is to show that for a path restricted to be close to another (deterministic) path for a while, its passage time is unlikely to be small (compared to that of a geodesic with the same endpoints).
We then use the FKG inequality to move from a deterministic path to a geodesic.

Lemma 8.11
.

For sufficiently small
c_{0}>0
, there is
c_{1}>0
, such that for
l\in\mathbb{N}
large enough (depending on
c_{0}
) and any
r\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{a,b\in\llbracket 0,c_{0}l^{2/3}\rrbracket}T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle l,r+b\rangle}\right]<4l-c_{1}l^{1/3}.

Proof.

Take
u=\langle-\lfloor c_{0}^{3/2}l\rfloor,0\rangle
and
v=\langle l+\lfloor c_{0}^{3/2}l\rfloor,r^{\prime}\rangle
, where
r^{\prime}
is the number in
\lfloor c_{0}l^{2/3}\rfloor\mathbb{Z}
with
r\leq r^{\prime}<r+\lfloor c_{0}l^{2/3}\rfloor
.
Note that

\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{a,b\in\llbracket 0,c_{0}l^{2/3}\rrbracket}T_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle l,r+b\rangle}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}[T_{u,v}]-\mathbb{E}\left[\min_{a\in\llbracket 0,c_{0}l^{2/3}\rrbracket}T_{u,\langle-1,a\rangle}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\min_{b\in\llbracket 0,c_{0}l^{2/3}\rrbracket}T_{\langle l+1,r+b\rangle,v}\right].

By Proposition
5.3
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\min_{a\in\llbracket 0,c_{0}l^{2/3}\rrbracket}T_{u,\langle-1,a\rangle}\right],\;\mathbb{E}\left[\min_{b\in\llbracket 0,c_{0}l^{2/3}\rrbracket}T_{\langle l+1,r+b\rangle,v}\right]\geq 4c_{0}^{3/2}l-Cc_{0}^{1/2}l^{1/3},

where
C>0
is a universal constant.
We also claim that for
l
sufficiently large,

\mathbb{E}[T_{u,v}]\leq 4(l+2c_{0}^{3/2}l)-c_{2}l^{1/3},

(8.7)

for some small universal constant
c_{2}>0
.
Let
C^{\prime}>0
be a large enough universal constant.
When
l^{-2/3}|r|>C^{\prime}
, (
8.7
) follows from (
5.3
).
When
l^{-2/3}|r|\leq C^{\prime}
, for each
l
there are at most
3C^{\prime}/c_{0}
possible numbers
r^{\prime}
can take. For each of them, by Theorem
8.7
the corresponding
T_{u,v}
after rescaling converges (as
l\to\infty
) to one point of the Airy
2
process, whose law is given by the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution.
Thus (
8.7
) (for
l
large enough) follows since the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution has negative expectation.
By choosing
c_{0}
such that
2Cc_{0}^{1/2}<c_{2}/2
and letting
c_{1}=c_{2}/2
, we complete the proof.
∎

For the next lemma, as before we denote
T_{u,v}^{\bullet}=T_{u,v}-\xi(v)
for any vertices
u\leq v
.

Lemma 8.12
.

For
l,M\in\mathbb{N}
and any
r_{0},\ldots,r_{M}\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{a_{0},\ldots,a_{M}\in\llbracket 0,cl^{2/3}\rrbracket}\sum_{i=0}^{M-1}T_{\langle il,r_{i}+a_{i}\rangle,\langle(i+1)l,r_{i+1}+a_{i+1}\rangle}^{\bullet}\geq 4Ml-cMl^{1/3}\right]<Ce^{-cM},

for some universal constants
c,C>0
, when
l
is large enough.

Proof.

In this proof we let
c,C>0
denote small and large enough universal constants, and their values can change from line to line.

Take
c_{0},c_{1}>0
such that Lemma
8.11
holds.
For each
0\leq i\leq M-1
we denote
S_{i}=\max_{a_{i},a_{i+1}\in\llbracket 0,c_{0}l^{2/3}\rrbracket}T_{\langle il,r_{i}+a_{i}\rangle,\langle(i+1)l,r_{i+1}+a_{i+1}\rangle}^{\bullet}
.
Then (by Lemma
8.11
) we have each
\mathbb{E}[S_{i}]<4l-c_{1}l^{1/3}
when
l
is large enough.

Next we apply Proposition
5.3
.
When
|r_{i}-r_{i+1}|\leq 0.9l
we could directly apply it; and when
|r_{i}-r_{i+1}|>0.9l
, the slope condition may not be satisfied, thus we use the fact that
T_{\langle il,r_{i}+a_{i}\rangle,\langle(i+1)l,r_{i+1}+a_{i+1}\rangle}^{\bullet}<T_{\langle il,r_{i}+a_{i}\rangle,\langle(i+1)l+\lfloor 0.1l\rfloor,r_{i+1}+a_{i+1}\rangle}^{\bullet}
, and upper bound the later using Proposition
5.3
.
In either case we conclude that

\mathbb{P}[S_{i}>4l+xl^{1/3}]<Ce^{-cx}
,
for any
x>0
.

Note that
S_{i}
for each
i
are independent.
Thus by a Bernstein type bound on the sum of independent random variables with exponential tails, we have

\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{a_{0},\ldots,a_{M}\in\llbracket 0,c_{0}l^{2/3}\rrbracket}\sum_{i=0}^{M-1}T_{\langle il,r_{i}+a_{i}\rangle,\langle(i+1)l,r_{i+1}+a_{i+1}\rangle}^{\bullet}\geq 4Ml-\frac{c_{1}}{2}Ml^{1/3}\right]<Ce^{-cM}.

Then the conclusion follows.
∎

Proof of Lemma
8.4
.

Take any up-right path
\Gamma
from
\mathbf{0}
to
\langle n,b\rangle
.
Denote
\mathcal{D}_{\Gamma}
as the following event: there exists an up-right path
\gamma
from
\mathbb{L}_{m}
to
\mathbb{L}_{m+Ml}
,
such that

•

\gamma
is disjoint from
\Gamma
.

•

The passage time of
\gamma
(i.e.
T(\gamma)
) is at least
4Ml-c_{0}Ml^{1/3}
.

•

For each
i=0,1,\ldots,M
,
|ad(\Gamma\cap\mathbb{L}_{m+il})-ad(\gamma\cap\mathbb{L}_{m+il})|<2c_{0}l^{2/3}
.

Here
c_{0}>0
is the same as in the definition of
\mathcal{D}_{M,l,m}^{\mathbf{0},\langle n,b\rangle}
.
Now we consider the event
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\langle n,b\rangle}=\Gamma
.
Under this event we have
\mathcal{D}_{M,l,m}^{\mathbf{0},\langle n,b\rangle}=\mathcal{D}_{\Gamma}
.
Also,
\Gamma_{u,v}=\Gamma
is a negative event of the field on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\Gamma
, while
\mathcal{D}_{\Gamma}
is determined by the field on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\Gamma
, and is a positive event of the field on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\Gamma
.
By the FKG inequality we have

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{D}_{M,l,m}^{\mathbf{0},\langle n,b\rangle}\mid\Gamma_{u,v}=\Gamma]=\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{D}_{\Gamma}\mid\Gamma_{u,v}=\Gamma]\leq\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{D}_{\Gamma}].

By Lemma
8.12
, we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{D}_{\Gamma}]<Ce^{-cM}
when
c_{0}<c
and
l>C
, for
c,C>0
being universal constants.
By averaging over all
\Gamma
we get the conclusion.
∎

9
Convergence of one point distribution

In this section we prove Theorems
1.3
and
1.4
.
The general idea is to show that the law of the environment around a specific vertex in the geodesic is close to that of nearby vertices along the geodesic; and this is achieved by a coalescing argument. Then we use Proposition
8.1
to argue that certain time average (of environments along the geodesic) is close to the stationary measure
\nu
.

To prove Theorem
1.3
, a key step would be to bound the total variation distance between
\xi\{\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i]\},\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}-\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i]
and
\xi\{\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i-r]\},\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}-\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i-r]
in a finite box, for any
i
large and
r
much smaller than
i
.
For this, we use translation invariance, and consider the environment around
\Gamma_{v[r]}[i-r]
instead of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i-r]
, where

v[r]=\begin{cases}\langle\lfloor r/2\rfloor,0\rangle&r\text{ is even};\\
\langle\lfloor r/2\rfloor,0\rangle+(1,0)&r\text{ is odd}.\end{cases}

We define
v[r]
this way so that there is always
d(v[r])=r
.
We show that with high probability
\Gamma_{v[r]}[i-r]=\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i]
, and in a finite box around this vertex the paths
\Gamma_{v[r]}
and
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
are the same.
Towards this we need the following estimate on coalescence of geodesics, which directly follows from Proposition
5.5
and Lemma
5.6
.

Lemma 9.1
.

There is a constant
C>0
, such that for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
, and
k>2
, we have

\mathbb{P}[\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor rk\rfloor}\neq\Gamma_{v[r]}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor rk\rfloor}]<C\log(k)k^{-2/3}
.

Proof.

Denote the intersections of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
and
\Gamma_{v[r]}
with
\mathbb{L}_{r}
as
\langle r,b_{r}\rangle
and
\langle r,b_{r}^{\prime}\rangle
, respectively.
By Lemma
5.6
and Proposition
5.5
, there is a constant
C_{0}>0
such that

\mathbb{P}[|b_{r}|,|b_{r}^{\prime}|\leq C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}]>1-C_{0}k^{-1},

and

\mathbb{P}[\Gamma_{\langle r,-\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor-1\rangle}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor rk\rfloor}\neq\Gamma_{\langle r,\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor+1\rangle}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor rk\rfloor}]<C_{0}^{2}\log(k)(k-1)^{-2/3}.

Thus the conclusion follows by ordering of geodesics (Lemma
5.1
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Take any
s\in\mathbb{N}
and any continuous function
f:\mathbb{R}^{\llbracket-s,s\rrbracket^{2}}\times\{0,1\}^{\llbracket-s,s\rrbracket^{2}}\to[0,1]
, regarded as a function on
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\times\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
. We need to show that

\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[f(\xi\{\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i]\},\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}-\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i])]=\nu(f)
.

For
i,r\in\mathbb{N}
and
k>2
with
i-2s>2rk
, by Lemma
9.1
, with probability at least
1-C\log(k)k^{-2/3}
we have
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[j]=\Gamma_{v[r]}[j-r]
for any
j\geq i-2s
; thus
\xi\{\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i]\},\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}-\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i]
and
\xi\{\Gamma_{v[r]}[i-r]\},\Gamma_{v[r]}-\Gamma_{v[r]}[i-r]
are the same in
\llbracket-s,s\rrbracket^{2}
.
Since
\xi\{\Gamma_{v[r]}[i-r]\},\Gamma_{v[r]}-\Gamma_{v[r]}[i-r]
have the same joint distribution as
\xi\{\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i-r]\},\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}-\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i-r]
, we must have that

|\mathbb{E}[f(\xi\{\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i]\},\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}-\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i])]-\mathbb{E}[f(\xi\{\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i-r],\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}-\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i-r]\})]|\leq C\log(k)k^{-2/3}.

By averaging over
r\in\llbracket 0,i/4k\rrbracket
, we have (when
i>4s
)

|\mathbb{E}[f(\xi\{\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i]\},\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}-\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i])]-\mathbb{E}[\mu_{\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i-\lfloor i/4k\rfloor],\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i]}(f)]|\leq C\log(k)k^{-2/3}.

By Lemma
5.6
, and Proposition
8.1
, for any fixed
k>0
, we have
\mu_{\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i-\lfloor i/4k\rfloor],\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i]}(f)\to\nu(f)
in probability as
i\to\infty
.
Thus we have that

\limsup_{i\to\infty}|\mathbb{E}[f(\xi\{\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i]\},\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}-\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}[i])]-\nu(f)|\leq C\log(k)k^{-2/3}.

Since
k
can be arbitrarily large, the conclusion follows.
∎

The proof of Theorem
1.4
is similar.
Again we need the following estimate on coalescence of geodesics,
which follows from Corollary
5.9
and Proposition
5.4
.
Recall that we denote
\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{n}^{\rho}=\langle n,0\rangle=\left(\left\lfloor\frac{2(1-\rho)^{2}n}{\rho^{2}+(1-\rho)^{2}}\right\rfloor,\left\lceil\frac{2\rho^{2}n}{\rho^{2}+(1-\rho)^{2}}\right\rceil\right)
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Lemma 9.2
.

There is a constant
C>0
, such that for any
r,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k>2
, with
n\geq 2rk
, we have
\mathbb{P}[\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor rk\rfloor}\neq\Gamma_{v[r],\mathbf{n}+v[r]}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor rk\rfloor}]<C\log(k)k^{-2/3}
, and
\mathbb{P}[\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}\cap\mathbb{L}_{n-\lfloor rk\rfloor}\neq\Gamma_{v[r],\mathbf{n}+v[r]}\cap\mathbb{L}_{n-\lfloor rk\rfloor}]<C\log(k)k^{-2/3}
.

\mathbb{L}_{r}
\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor rk\rfloor}
\mathbb{L}_{n-r}
\mathbf{0}
\mathbf{n}+v[r]
\mathbf{n}
v[r]
\langle r,-\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor-1\rangle
\langle n-r,-\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor-1\rangle
\langle r,\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor+1\rangle
\langle n-r,\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor+1\rangle

Figure 18
:
An illustration of the proof of Lemma
9.2
.
The geodesics
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}
and
\Gamma_{v[r],\mathbf{n}+v[r]}
are sandwiched between
\Gamma_{\langle r,-\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor-1\rangle,\langle n-r,-\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor-1\rangle}
and
\Gamma_{\langle r,\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor+1\rangle,\langle n-r,\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor+1\rangle}
.

Proof.

Since
n\geq 2rk
, we just show

\mathbb{P}[\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor rk\rfloor}\neq\Gamma_{v[r],\mathbf{n}+v[r]}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor rk\rfloor}]<C\log(k)k^{-2/3}
, and by symmetry the other inequality would follow.

Denote the intersections of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}
and
\Gamma_{v[r],\mathbf{n}+v[r]}
with
\mathbb{L}_{r}
as
\langle r,b_{-}\rangle
and
\langle r,b_{-}^{\prime}\rangle
, respectively;
and the intersections of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}
and
\Gamma_{v[r],\mathbf{n}+v[r]}
with
\mathbb{L}_{n-r}
as
\langle n-r,b_{+}\rangle
and
\langle n-r,b_{+}^{\prime}\rangle
, respectively.
There is a constant
C_{0}>0
, such that

\mathbb{P}[|b_{-}|,|b_{-}^{\prime}|\leq C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}],\;\mathbb{P}[|b_{+}|,|b_{+}^{\prime}|\leq C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}]>1-C_{0}k^{-1}

by Corollary
5.9
;
and

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}[\Gamma_{\langle r,-\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor-1\rangle,\langle n-r,-\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor-1\rangle}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor rk\rfloor}\neq\Gamma_{\langle r,\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor+1\rangle,\langle n-r,\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor+1\rangle}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor rk\rfloor}]\\
\leq&\mathbb{P}[\Gamma_{\langle r,-\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor-1\rangle,\langle n-r,-\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor-1\rangle}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor rk\rfloor}\neq\Gamma_{\langle r,-\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor-1\rangle,\langle n-r,\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor+1\rangle}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor rk\rfloor}]\\
&+\mathbb{P}[\Gamma_{\langle r,-\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor-1\rangle,\langle n-r,\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor+1\rangle}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor rk\rfloor}\neq\Gamma_{\langle r,\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor+1\rangle,\langle n-r,\lfloor C_{0}\log(k)r^{2/3}\rfloor+1\rangle}\cap\mathbb{L}_{\lfloor rk\rfloor}]\\
<&C_{0}^{2}\log(k)(k-1)^{-2/3},\end{split}

where the last inequality is by Proposition
5.4
. Then the conclusion follows by ordering of geodesics (Lemma
5.1
). See Figure
18
for an illustration.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Take any
s\in\mathbb{N}
and any continuous function
f:\mathbb{R}^{\llbracket-s,s\rrbracket^{2}}\times\{0,1\}^{\llbracket-s,s\rrbracket^{2}}\to[0,1]
, regarded as a function on
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\times\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
. We need to show that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[f(\xi\{\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}[\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor]\},\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}-\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}[\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor])]=\nu(f).

Without loss of generality we assume that
\alpha\leq 1
.
For
n,r\in\mathbb{N}
and
k>2
with
\alpha n-2s>2rk
and
\alpha n+2s<2n-2rk
, by Lemma
9.2
we have

\mathbb{P}[\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}[\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor+j]=\Gamma_{v[r],\mathbf{n}+v[r]}[\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor-r+j]\},\;\forall j\in\llbracket-2s,2s\rrbracket]\geq 1-C\log(k)k^{-2/3}.

By translation invariance,
\xi\{\Gamma_{v[r],\mathbf{n}+v[r]}[\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor-r]\},\Gamma_{v[r],\mathbf{n}+v[r]}-\Gamma_{v[r],\mathbf{n}+v[r]}[\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor-r]
have the same joint distribution as
\xi\{\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}[\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor-r]\},\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}-\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}[\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor-r]
. So we must have that

|\mathbb{E}[f(\xi\{\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}[\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor]\},\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}-\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}[\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor])]-\mathbb{E}[f(\xi\{\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}[\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor-r]\},\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}-\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}[\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor-r])]|\leq C\log(k)k^{-2/3}.

By averaging over
r\in\llbracket 0,\alpha n/4k\rrbracket
, we have (when
\alpha n>4s
)

|\mathbb{E}[f(\xi\{\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}[\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor]\},\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}-\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}[\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor])]-\mathbb{E}[\mu_{\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}[\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor-\lfloor\alpha n/4k\rfloor],\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}[\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor]}(f)]|\leq C\log(k)k^{-2/3}.

By Corollary
5.9
and Proposition
8.1
, for fixed
k
we have
\mu_{\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}[\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor-\lfloor\alpha n/4k\rfloor],\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}[\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor]}(f)\to\nu(f)
in probability as
n\to\infty
.
Thus we have that

\limsup_{i\to\infty}|\mathbb{E}[f(\xi\{\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}[\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor]\},\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}-\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}[\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor])]-\nu(f)|\leq C\log(k)k^{-2/3}.

Then the conclusion follows since
k
can be arbitrarily large.
∎

10
Exponential concentration via counting argument

Using a covering argument, we can prove the following exponential concentration of the empirical environment, for both finite and semi-infinite geodesics.

Proposition 10.1
.

For any
s\in\mathbb{N}
, and any bounded continuous
f:\mathbb{R}^{\llbracket-s,s\rrbracket^{2}}\times\{0,1\}^{\llbracket-s,s\rrbracket^{2}}\to\mathbb{R}
, regarded as a function on
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\times\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
, and any
\epsilon>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}[|\mu_{\mathbf{0};r}(f)-\nu(f)|>\epsilon]<Ce^{-cr},

for
r
large enough, and
c,C>0
depending on
s,f,\epsilon
.

Proposition 10.2
.

Let
\{b_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of integers such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}n^{-2/3}|b_{n}|<\infty
.
Then for any
s\in\mathbb{N}
, any bounded continuous
f:\mathbb{R}^{\llbracket-s,s\rrbracket^{2}}\times\{0,1\}^{\llbracket-s,s\rrbracket^{2}}\to\mathbb{R}
, regarded as a function on
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\times\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
, and any
\epsilon>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}[|\mu_{\mathbf{0},\langle n,b_{n}\rangle}(f)-\nu(f)|>\epsilon]<Ce^{-cn},

for
n
large enough, and
c,C>0
depending on
s,f,\epsilon
.

From Proposition
10.1
we can deduce Theorem
1.2
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

By Proposition
10.1
, for any bounded continuous
f:\mathbb{R}^{\llbracket-s,s\rrbracket^{2}}\times\{0,1\}^{\llbracket-s,s\rrbracket^{2}}\to\mathbb{R}
(regarded as a function on
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\times\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
) and
\epsilon>0
, we have that
\sum_{r\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{P}[|\mu_{\mathbf{0};r}(f)-\nu(f)|>\epsilon]<\infty
.
So almost surely, there exists some (random)
r_{0}
such that
|\mu_{\mathbf{0};r}(f)-\nu(f)|\leq\epsilon
for any
r>r_{0}
.
Thus we have that
\mu_{\mathbf{0};r}(f)\to\nu(f)
almost surely.
The conclusion follows by taking all
s\in\mathbb{N}
, and
f
over
a countable dense subset of the space of continuous and compactly supported functions on
\mathbb{R}^{\llbracket-s,s\rrbracket^{2}}\times\{0,1\}^{\llbracket-s,s\rrbracket^{2}}
with the uniform convergence topology.
∎

Using the same arguments we can deduce Theorem
1.1
from Proposition
10.2
. We omit the details.

To prove these exponential concentration bounds (Propositions
10.1
and
10.2
), we cover the geodesics with short finite ones, and use Proposition
8.1
.

We take
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
m^{2/3}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
For each
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
we denote
L_{i,j}
as the segment joining
\langle im,(2j-1)m^{2/3}\rangle
and
\langle im,(2j+1)m^{2/3}\rangle
.
For each integer sequence
j_{0},j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}
, we let
P_{j_{0},\ldots,j_{k}}
be the collection of paths from
L_{0,j_{0}}
to
L_{k,j_{k}}
, intersecting each
L_{i,j_{i}}
,
0\leq i\leq k
.
For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
D>0
, we denote
P_{k,D}
as the union of all
P_{j_{0},j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}}
such that
j_{0}=0
and
\sum_{i=1}^{k}(j_{i}-j_{i-1})^{2}>Dk
.
In words,
P_{k,D}
contains all paths from
L_{0,0}
to
\mathbb{L}_{km}
with ‘quadratic variation’
>Dk
.
We next upper bound the passage times of these paths.

Lemma 10.3
.

There exists
c_{0}>0
, such that when
m,k,D
are large enough,

\mathbb{P}\bigg[\exists\gamma\in P_{k,D},T(\gamma)>\frac{2km}{(1-\rho)^{2}+\rho^{2}}-(b_{+}-b_{-})(\rho^{-1}-(1-\rho)^{-1})-c_{0}Dkm^{1/3}\bigg]<e^{-c_{0}k},

where
b_{-},b_{+}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\langle 0,b_{-}\rangle,\langle km,b_{+}\rangle
are the intersections of
\gamma
with
\mathbb{L}_{0},\mathbb{L}_{km}
, respectively.

Proof.

First, there exist
c_{1},C_{1}>0
such that for
m
large enough and any
j\in\mathbb{Z}
,
x>0
,

\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\langle 0,b\rangle\in L_{0,0},\\
\langle m,b^{\prime}\rangle\in L_{1,j}\end{subarray}}T_{\langle 0,b\rangle,\langle m,b^{\prime}\rangle}+(b^{\prime}-b)(\rho^{-1}-(1-\rho)^{-1})\Bigg]<\frac{2m}{(1-\rho)^{2}+\rho^{2}}+(C_{1}-c_{1}j^{2})m^{1/3},

\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\langle 0,b\rangle\in L_{0,0},\\
\langle m,b^{\prime}\rangle\in L_{1,j}\end{subarray}}T_{\langle 0,b\rangle,\langle m,b^{\prime}\rangle}+(b^{\prime}-b)(\rho^{-1}-(1-\rho)^{-1})>\frac{2m}{(1-\rho)^{2}+\rho^{2}}+(x-c_{1}j^{2})m^{1/3}\Bigg]<C_{1}e^{-c_{1}x}.

When
|j|<(\rho^{2}\wedge(1-\rho)^{2})m^{1/3}
these inequalities follow from Proposition
5.3
and (
5.3
), and fundamental computations. When
|j|\geq(\rho^{2}\wedge(1-\rho)^{2})m^{1/3}
these inequalities can be obtained by applying (
5.1
) in Theorem
5.2
to each
T_{u,v}
with
u\in L_{0,0}
and
v\in L_{1,j}
and taking a union bound.

Note that

\max_{\gamma\in P_{j_{0},j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}}}T(\gamma)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in L_{i-1,j_{i-1}},\\
v\in L_{i,j_{i}}\end{subarray}}T_{u,v}^{\bullet}+\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in L_{k-1,j_{k-1}},\\
v\in L_{k,j_{k}}\end{subarray}}T_{u,v}.

Here
T_{u,v}^{\bullet}=T_{u,v}-\xi(v)
for any
u\leq v\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
Then by a Bernstein type estimate for independent random variables with exponential tails, we have

\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{\gamma\in P_{j_{0},j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}}}T(\gamma)+(b_{+}-b_{-})(\rho^{-1}-(1-\rho)^{-1})>\frac{2km}{(1-\rho)^{2}+\rho^{2}}-\frac{c_{1}}{2}Dkm^{1/3}\right]\\
<C_{2}e^{-c_{2}\sum_{i=1}^{k}(j_{i}-j_{i-1})^{2}},

for any
D
large (depending on
c_{1},C_{1}
) and any integer sequence
j_{0},\ldots,j_{k}
with
j_{0}=0
,
\sum_{i=1}^{k}(j_{i}-j_{i-1})^{2}>Dk
.
Here
c_{2},C_{2}>0
are constants, and
\langle 0,b_{-}\rangle,\langle km,b_{+}\rangle
are the intersections of
\gamma
with
\mathbb{L}_{0},\mathbb{L}_{km}
.
Summing over all such sequences
j_{0},j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}
, the right-hand side is bounded by

C_{2}e^{-c_{2}Dk/2}\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-c_{2}j^{2}/2}\right)^{k}.

By taking
D
large so that
e^{c_{2}D/4}>\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-c_{2}j^{2}/2}
, we get the conclusion.
∎

We next prove Proposition
10.1
. The general idea is to upper bound the ‘quadratic variation’ of the first
r
steps of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
, and use Proposition
8.1
to show that the empirical environment between each
\mathbb{L}_{im}
and
\mathbb{L}_{(i+1)m}
is close to
\nu
, and use independence to deduce the exponential concentration.

Proof of Proposition
10.1
.

For any vertices
u\leq v
, denote

\mu_{u,v}^{\bullet}:=\frac{1}{|\Gamma_{u,v}|-1}\sum_{w\in\Gamma_{u,v},w\neq v}\delta_{(\xi\{w\},\Gamma_{u,v}-w)},

i.e. it is the empirical environment along
\Gamma_{u,v}
, excluding the last vertex
v
.
Without loss of generality we assume that
0\leq f\leq 1
, and
\epsilon
is small enough (depending on
s
and
f
).

We first consider paths with small ‘quadratic variation’. Take
D>0
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
m^{2/3}\in\mathbb{Z}
, and let them be large enough as required by Lemma
10.3
. We also choose
m
large enough such that

\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{|a|,|b|<\epsilon^{-2}m^{2/3}}|\mu_{\langle 0,a\rangle,\langle m,b\rangle}^{\bullet}(f)-\nu(f)|>\epsilon^{2}\right]<\varepsilon,

(10.1)

by Proposition
8.1
.
Here
\varepsilon
is a small number depending on
D,\epsilon
and to be determined.
Take any
k\in\mathbb{N}
(also large enough as required by Lemma
10.3
), and a sequence
j_{0},\ldots,j_{k}
such that
j_{0}=0
and
\sum_{i=1}^{k}(j_{i}-j_{i-1})^{2}\leq Dk
.
We let
I^{\prime}\subset\{1,\ldots,k\}
be the collection of indices such that

|j_{i}-j_{i-1}|<\epsilon^{-2}/2-1
for each
i\in I^{\prime}
. Then
|I^{\prime}|>(1-\epsilon/2)k
, when
\epsilon
is small enough (depending on
D
).
Next we let
I\subset I^{\prime}
such that for each
i\in I
,

\max_{u\in L_{i-1,j_{i-1}},v\in L_{i,j_{i}}}|\mu_{u,v}^{\bullet}(f)-\nu(f)|\leq\epsilon^{2}.

By (
10.1
) we have
\mathbb{P}[i\in I]>1-\varepsilon
for each
i\in I^{\prime}
.
Also note that
i_{1}\in I
and
i_{2}\in I
are independent for any
i_{1},i_{2}\in I^{\prime}
with
i_{1}-i_{2}\geq 2
.
Then by a Chernoff bound and taking
\varepsilon
small enough (depending on
D,\epsilon
), we can make
\mathbb{P}[|I^{\prime}|-|I|>\epsilon^{2}k]<(D+1)^{-2k}
.

Let
\gamma
be the path consisting of the first
2km+1
vertices of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
;
i.e.
\gamma
is the part of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
on and between
\mathbb{L}_{0}
and
\mathbb{L}_{km}
.
Given that
\gamma\in P_{j_{0},\ldots,j_{k}}
, and
|I^{\prime}|-|I|\leq\epsilon^{2}k
,
for any
r\in\llbracket 2km,2(k+1)m\rrbracket
we must have that
|\mu_{\mathbf{0};r}(f)-\nu(f)|\leq\epsilon/2+2\epsilon^{2}+1/(k+1)
.
So when
k>\epsilon^{-2}
and
\epsilon
is large enough, we have

\mathbb{P}\left[\gamma\in P_{j_{0},\ldots,j_{k}},|\mu_{\mathbf{0};r}(f)-\nu(f)|>\epsilon\right]<(D+1)^{-2k}.

Thus by summing over all sequences
j_{0},\ldots,j_{k}
with
j_{0}=0
,
\sum_{i=1}^{k}(j_{i}-j_{i-1})^{2}\leq Dk
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left[\gamma\not\in P_{k,D},|\mu_{\mathbf{0};r}(f)-\nu(f)|>\epsilon\right]<{\lfloor Dk\rfloor+k-1\choose k-1}(D+1)^{-2k}<e^{-ck}

for some
c>0
depending on
D
.

Now it remains to bound
\mathbb{P}[\gamma\in P_{k,D}]
.
By Lemma
10.3
, we have

\mathbb{P}[\gamma\in P_{k,D}]<e^{-c_{0}k}+\mathbb{P}\bigg[T(\gamma)\leq\frac{2km}{(1-\rho)^{2}+\rho^{2}}-b_{+}(\rho^{-1}-(1-\rho)^{-1})-c_{0}Dkm^{1/3}\bigg],

(10.2)

where
\langle km,b_{+}\rangle
is the intersection of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
with
\mathbb{L}_{km}
, and recall that
c_{0}>0
is a constant independent of
m,k,D
.
When the event in the right-hand side of (
10.2
) happens, we must have that (at least) one of the following happens:

•

|b_{+}|>km^{2/3}
,

•

\max_{|b|\leq km^{2/3}}\mathbf{B}(\langle km,b\rangle,\langle km,0\rangle)-b(\rho^{-1}-(1-\rho)^{-1})\geq c_{0}Dkm^{1/3}/3
,

•

T_{\mathbf{0},\langle km,0\rangle}^{\bullet}\leq\frac{2km}{(1-\rho)^{2}+\rho^{2}}-c_{0}Dkm^{1/3}/2
,

where
T_{u,v}^{\bullet}=T_{u,v}-\xi(v)
for any vertices
u\leq v
as before, and recall that
\mathbf{B}
is the Busemann function (defined in Section
3.1
).
To see this, we assume the contrary, i.e. none of the above three events happen (while the event in the right-hand side of (
10.2
) happens).
Then we must have
T_{\mathbf{0},\langle km,0\rangle}^{\bullet}-T_{\mathbf{0},\langle km,0\rangle}^{\bullet}>\mathbf{B}(\langle km,b_{+}\rangle,\langle km,0\rangle)
, which contradicts with Lemma
3.1
.

We claim that we can bound the probability of each of the three events by
C^{\prime}e^{-c^{\prime}k}
, for some
c^{\prime},C^{\prime}>0
depending on
m,D
.
For the first event the bound is by Lemma
5.6
.
For the second event, note that
b\mapsto\mathbf{B}(\langle km,b\rangle,\langle km,0\rangle)-b(\rho^{-1}-(1-\rho)^{-1})
is a (two-sided) centered random walk; for the third event, use Theorem
5.2
.

Finally, by sequentially choosing
D,\epsilon,\varepsilon,m
, and considering all large enough
k
and each
r\in\llbracket 2km,2(k+1)m\rrbracket
, the conclusion follows.
∎

We prove Proposition
10.2
using a similar strategy.

Proof of Proposition
10.2
.

The first half of this proof follows the same way as the proof of Proposition
10.1
.
We omit the details, and conclude that the following is true for any
D>0
,
\epsilon>0
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m^{2/3}\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
D,m
are large enough as required by Lemma
10.3
,
\epsilon
is small enough depending on
D
, and
m
is large enough depending on
D,\epsilon
.
Take any
k\in\mathbb{N}
which is
>\epsilon^{-2}
and large enough as required by Lemma
10.3
, and take any
n\in\llbracket km,(k+1)m\rrbracket
.
Let
\gamma
be the path from
\mathbb{L}_{0}
to
\mathbb{L}_{km}
, consisting of the first
2km+1
vertices of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\langle n,b_{n}\rangle}
.
Then we have

\mathbb{P}\left[\gamma\not\in P_{k,D},|\mu_{\mathbf{0},\langle n,b_{n}\rangle}(f)-\nu(f)|>\epsilon\right]<{\lfloor Dk\rfloor+k-1\choose k-1}(D+1)^{-2k}<e^{-ck}

for some
c>0
depending on
D
.
It remains to bound
\mathbb{P}[\gamma\in P_{k,D}]
.
By Lemma
10.3
, we have

\mathbb{P}[\gamma\in P_{k,D}]<e^{-c_{0}k}+\mathbb{P}\bigg[T(\gamma)\leq\frac{2km}{(1-\rho)^{2}+\rho^{2}}-b_{+}(\rho^{-1}-(1-\rho)^{-1})-c_{0}Dkm^{1/3}\bigg],

(10.3)

where
\langle km,b_{+}\rangle
is the intersection of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\langle n,b_{n}\rangle}
with
\mathbb{L}_{km}
.
When the event in the right-hand side of (
10.3
) happens, we must have that (at least) one of the following happens:

•

\max_{b\in\mathbb{Z}}T_{\langle km,b\rangle,\langle n,b_{n}\rangle}-(b-b_{n})(\rho^{-1}-(1-\rho)^{-1})\geq c_{0}Dkm^{1/3}/3
,

•

T_{\mathbf{0},\langle km,b_{n}\rangle}\leq\frac{2km}{(1-\rho)^{2}+\rho^{2}}-b_{n}(\rho^{-1}-(1-\rho)^{-1})-c_{0}Dkm^{1/3}/2
.

To see this, we assume the contrary, i.e. none of the above events happen.
Then we must have

T(\gamma)>T_{\mathbf{0},\langle n,b_{n}\rangle}-T_{\langle km,b_{+}\rangle,\langle n,b_{n}\rangle}\geq T_{\mathbf{0},\langle km,b_{n}\rangle}-T_{\langle km,b_{+}\rangle,\langle n,b_{n}\rangle}\\
>\frac{2km}{(1-\rho)^{2}+\rho^{2}}-b_{+}(\rho^{-1}-(1-\rho)^{-1})-5c_{0}Dkm^{1/3}/6,

which contradicts with the event in the right-hand side of (
10.3
).
We claim that we can bound the probability of each of the two events by
C^{\prime}e^{-c^{\prime}k}
, for some
c^{\prime},C^{\prime}>0
depending on
m,D
.
For the first event,
note that
n-km\leq m
, then the bound can be obtained by taking a union bound over all up-right paths from
\mathbb{L}_{km}
to
\langle n,b_{n}\rangle
(there are at most
2^{2m}
such paths, and the passage time of each is the sum of at most
2m+1
i.i.d.
\Exp(1)
random variables).
For the second event, apply Theorem
5.2
.
Thus the conclusion follows.
∎
